REGULAR MEETING
August 5, 2026

Broadway, in Wichita, Kansas, by Chairman Jeff Blubaugh, with the following present: Chair Pro-Tem Commissioner Stephanie Wise; Commissioner Peter F. Meitzner; Commissioner Ryan Baty; Commissioner James M. ROLL CALL
The Clerk reported, after calling roll, that all Commissioners were present.
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Next item, please.”
COUNTY ANNOUNCEMENTS
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Good morning, Nicole.”
Ms. Nicole Gibbs,
Community Relations Director, greeted the Commissioners and said, “First off, we are going to have the Treasurer, Brandi Baily.”
VISUAL PRESENATION
Ms. Gibbs
continued, “She’s here to give an update on Tag Office operations. Ma’am.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Good morning, Brandi.”
Ms. Brandi Baily,
County Treasurer, greeted the Commissioners and said, “I guess I’m just so excited to see everybody here to hear about the Tag Office update today. It’s encouraging to me. Okay, first, I’d like to take a few minutes to provide an update on the Treasurer’s Tag Office, including our current operations, satellite office expansions, appointment availability, WaitTime system, and July transaction volume. First, I want to update you on the Derby Tag Office. We are now fully opened and serving customers Monday through Friday, 9:00 a.m. to 5:00 p.m. We did open up our offices on Monday.”
VISUAL PRESENTATON
Ms. Baily
continued, “At this time, we have one clerk station operating at that location with space set up and available for a second station. The limiting factor right now is staffing. Once we are able to staff that second position, we will be able to expand service capacity at that site. We are also in discussions about adding a more enclosed service area around this workstation we’ve been provided for better privacy for the customers and our staff. We’ve been working with the Derby Library Director on this and looking at possible options for that space and Eric, at the Derby library, he’s been wonderful to with. I just want to give him a shout-out and thanks for all his help. We have evaluated the possibility of purchasing or leasing a separate building in Derby. At this point, based on current needs and available resources, the best path forward is to continue expanding satellite locations throughout the county. That approach allows us to increase access, reduce pressure on the main office, and to bring services closer to residents in different parts of Sedgwick County.”
Ms. Baily
continued, “We are continuing to make progress on additional satellite offices. We have secured another workstation at one of our existing eastside satellite Tag Office locations, and we have also secured a workstation at a new location in western Sedgwick County. Those locations will be opened and announced once staffing is in place. We will not open a location before we are prepared to operate it effectively. Our goal is not to just add locations, but to make sure each location is properly staffed and capable of providing reliable services to the public. Regarding our WaitTime system, we have made improvements, and the system is more dialed in than accurately before. However, with that said, we know that there is still work to be done with this system. At this point, the best long-term solution is not simply purchasing another off-the-shelf solution. Our operations have specific needs, and we need a system that can be built around the way our offices actually function. We are continuing to evaluate options that would give us better accuracy, better reporting, and better customer experience.”
Ms. Baily
continued, “We continue to see strong demand for appointments, and we’ve identified opportunities to improve appointment availability for our customers. Currently, our appointment calendars allow customers to book up to 45 days in advance. Each day, a new date becomes available on the calendar, and those appointment slots are being filled very quickly. At this time, we are fully booked with appointments. We want to encourage the public to continue checking for cancellations. When someone cancels an appointment, the time slot does reopen and becomes available to another customer. We also want to make a direct request to the public: please do not book multiple appointments just in case. We’ve noticed there is an individual that’s out there that does have six appointment slots booked at the moment. When one person reserves several appointment times, it prevents our residents from being able to schedule this service. We need those appointment slots to remain available for customers who are ready and able to use the system right now. At the Douglas Tag Office alone, we are currently offering 105 appointment slots each day. We are using the capacity we have, but demand continues to be extremely high.”
Ms. Baily
continued, “Our goal is to get to the point where all title work is done either by appointment or by drop-off. I also want to remind residents that tag renewals to not require an appointment, nor do they require you to get in the queue line. Residents can walk into the Douglas Tag Office at any time during the business day and complete a renewal. The average wait time for a renewal right now is currently approximately 15 to 20 minutes. We want to make that residents understand that appointments are not the only option, especially for routine renewals. If you simply need to renew your tag, you can come directly into the office and get in line, 15-, 20-minute wait, and we will help you right then.”
Ms. Baily
continued, “Now, a brief update on our July numbers. In the month of July, the Treasurer’s Tag Office completed a total of 66,903 transactions. 40,885 of those were in-person transactions. 26,018 were web transactions. That’s an average of approximately 2,909 transactions a day. In terms of foot traffic, we served 21,276 people in person during the month of July. In the Douglas office alone, we saw 15,273 people. In the satellite offices, we saw 6,003 people. That means we are averaging approximately 925 people walking through our offices each day. For comparison, when we had both Douglas office and Kellogg office operating, we were typically seeing between 650 and 700 people a day. We are now serving significantly more people daily through the Douglas office and our satellite locations. That increase shows two things very clearly. First, demand for Tag Office services remains very high. Second, the satellite office model is helping us serve more customers across the county.”
Ms. Baily
continued, “Overall, we are continuing to focus on increasing access, improving efficiency, and making responsible decisions on how we expand our services. Staffing remains one of our biggest factors in how quickly we can open additional stations and locations, but the direction for the satellite offices are helping us reach more residents, reduce the burden on a single central location, and provide more convenient options to the public. We still have work to do, especially with appointments, wait times, and staffing, but we are making progress. Our goal is to continue building a Tag Office operation that is accessible, efficient, and responsive to the needs of Sedgwick County. With that, I will stand for any questions.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Brandi, thank you very much for your explanation. I know it’s very fluid. I know you’re making a lot of changes over there and trying to, you know, offer better efficiency. I know, you know, you guys have been out there hands-on, trying to take on the wait times and everything yourself. So appreciate it. Commissioner Howell, you have some questions?”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Yeah, thank you, Chairman. I just wanted to reflect on a couple things you said. I want to say thank you to Eric specifically, the Director of the Derby Library. He was very accommodating and very great to work with. He’s just a really nice guy. I also want to thank the City Council of the city of Derby and the Mayor especially for their advocacy and their interest in partnership with the county. They recognized this need, and they really stepped up. So I want to say thank you to them specifically. I also want to say thank you to the state legislature because they passed a law this last year which is going to provide additional revenue to your office. So again, all the challenges we’ve had going backwards I think they’re going to be a lot better going forward because we have a slightly better revenue stream that hopefully hire all the staff that you need to take care of business. So thanks to those three.”
Commissioner Howell
continued, “Then finally, I just wanted to say that, you know, when the announcement came out that the Tag Office in Derby was closing, a lot of people were confused because we actually were co-officing with the Kansas Driver’s License Office. A lot of people were upset because they wanted the Driver’s License Office not to close. They didn’t understand. Maybe they didn’t read the story. So when I explained to people that it’s really the tag services that are in the Driver’s License Office, and we’re talking about 35 appointments per day that’ll be reduced at that location, but we’re looking for another location. I kept saying, we’re looking for another location. Again, I know I’m not doing, but you’re doing it, and you did a great job, by the way. Without even a day of interruption, we went from one to the other with no interruption. Every appointment was kept. Still at least, I think, again, at least 35 per day. Maybe you’ll even ramp that up, I assume.”
Commissioner Howell
continued, “Rather than call this, just my recommendation is rather than call this a Tag Office, which people don’t understand that term, is tag services in the Derby Library. That, to me, is a little bit clearer, because if we decide to change it again in the future, and I think actually we should, I’d like to see a more permanent location that’s maybe more robust, more services being offered up potentially in a different and maybe even a better location. So I think we’re going to continue to try to solve that problem, but when it changes again, I don’t want people to get mad that we’re closing, you know, the library or something like that. So, anyway, I just want to say thank for all you did, and many people, actually, I think, should be thanked and remembered for actually really a good outcome for what needed to happen. So I just want to say thank you, Brandi, for what you did.”
Ms. Baily
said, “Thank you.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Commissioner Baty.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “Yeah. Brandi, thanks for being here today. I think a lot of people maybe misunderstand the relationship between the Commission and the Treasurer’s Office. So, can you give us an update on the staffing. This is how we can best support your office. How do we help you in the staffing piece of this?”
Ms. Baily
said, “Currently, for my frontline staff, as of Monday, I was fully staffed there. Friday, I did get additional approving to add five positions to my staffing table. So, those positions on Monday were posted out there. So, I do have five positions that are now out there available to add additional staffing to my staffing table.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “Okay.”
Ms. Baily
said, “As the funding has now increased from the state, we know that January 1st, there will be additional funding coming, and so then I can come back and ask for additional staffing to the table at that point when the funds are available. At this point, I had funds to essentially cover five staff, and that’s what’s posted out there to hire. Once I have those positions hired, then the intent is for the second station there in Derby Office, have that one completely full, and like I just announced, I have another location secured at one of our existing spots, and I have a brand new location out in western Sedgwick County that’s ready for us to go, and we’re just waiting on the staff. So as soon as those positions are full, and we have our people trained up, then those will be there. So, staffing right now, we’re looking good at the moment.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “Okay. Thank you, Brandi.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Brandi, thank you very much for coming in this morning. Appreciate it.”
Ms. Baily
said, “Thank you.”
Ms. Gibbs
said, “Okay. For County Good News, I’m going to have Stephen Shaughnessy come to the podium. The Central Plains Area Agency on Aging (CPAAA), which is housed here in Sedgwick County, recently received the Innovations Award from U.S. Aging during its annual conference. Stephen’s here to tell you more about it.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Good morning, Stephen.”
Mr. Stephen Shaughnessy,
Director of Aging, greeted the Commissioners and said, “We are thrilled to announce that our in-home counseling and community support groups program received the 2026 Aging Innovations Award from U.S. Aging, which is one the highest honors presented to its members.”
VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Shaughnessy
continued, “This program addresses mental health concerns and caregiver stress through comprehensive clinical assessment and the provision of in-home or community-based counseling sessions. Additionally, it delivers community-based psychoeducation and outreach to promote mental wellness, reduce stigma associated with therapy and treatment, and increase wellness of mental health risk factors. The client-centered program provides individual counseling and group therapy for adults ages 60 and older to their family caregivers and was among 16 area agency programs to receive the Innovations Award during the U.S. Aging’s 51st Annual Conference.”
Ms. Shaughnessy
continued, “It was my honor and privilege to receive the award on behalf of CPAAA at the Aging Conference a couple weeks ago, but today I want to recognize and thank Jennifer Lasley, Director of Information and Community Services, and Jessica Huber, Aging Specialist and Licensed Therapist, without whom this program would not have been possible. Jessica, who wasn’t able to be here today because she had an appointment with a client, helped develop the counseling program and the caregiver components of the program, and supports older adults as they work through life’s challenges, and taking steps towards improved mental wellness, and working with caregivers in the support group. To Jennifer who provides support and guidance to Jessica and provides the knowledge and expertise of the Older Americans Act to ensure funding is possible through this grant. Just want to thank the Commission for allowing us to be here and sharing this significant achievement for our department.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Well, congratulations and thank you very much. Commissioners, do you guys have any comments or questions?”

PROCLAMATIONS
E.
PROCLAMATION DECLARING NATIONAL EMERGENCY MANAGEMENT AWARENESS MONTH.
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Commissioner Howell will be giving this proclamation this morning.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Thank you, Mr. Chairman. This is a core function of Sedgwick County government, so I’m pleased to bring some recognition to your team. The proclamation says:
PROCLAMATION
WHEREAS,
local emergency managers, community leaders, State governments, and the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) dedicate countless hours to developing preparedness, response, recovery, and mitigation programs to protect the lives and property of all persons living in the United States and U.S. territories; and
WHEREAS, emergency managers across the United States and U.S. territories are charged with establishing and maintaining the capabilities necessary to effectively direct, coordinate, and support emergency and disaster response and recovery efforts; and
WHEREAS, the emergency managers serve the nation by responding to countless emergencies and disasters each year, demonstrating commitment, compassion, and professionalism in protecting the public; and
WHEREAS, the efforts of our nation’s emergency managers have helped millions of community members across the United States recover, rebuild, and become more resilient in the face of adversity.
NOW, THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED
that I, Chairman Jeff Blubaugh, and the Board of Sedgwick County Commissioners, do hereby proclaim August 2026 as
“NATIONAL EMERGENCY MANAGEMENT AWARENESS MONTH”
in conjunction with National Preparedness Month in September and urge all community members to express their sincere appreciation for emergency managers in every sector who work diligently to prepare, protect, and serve against all risks and hazards.
MOTION
Commissioner Howell moved to adopt the proclamation.
Chairman Blubaugh seconded the motion.
There was no discussion on the motion, the vote was called.
VOTE
Commissioner Meitzner
Commissioner Baty
Commissioner Howell
Commissioner Wise
Chairman Blubaugh
Aye
Aye
Aye
Aye
Aye
County Clerk Assigned Resolution Number 141-2026.
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Julie Stimson will be accepting.”
Ms. Julie Stimson,
Director of Emergency Management, greeted the Commission and said, “On behalf of Sedgwick County Emergency Management Team, thank you for recognizing August as National Emergency Management Awareness Month, this is the second annual event, and for your continued support of our mission. Emergency Management is much more than responding to disasters. Every day, our team works to strengthen preparedness before disasters strike and then coordinate response and recovery support when disasters do occur. In Sedgwick County, we serve more than 500,000 people in 20 cities by bringing together local governments, first responders, healthcare organizations, schools, volunteer organizations, private and public industry, and then state and federal partners before, during, and after disasters. Whether we are preparing for tornadoes, floods, wildfires, hazardous materials incidents, cyber threats, or supporting large community events, our success depends on collaboration and dedication of many partners.”
Ms. Stimson
continued, “I do want to recognize my Emergency Management Staff who’s here with me today: Cody Charvat, Bryan Ellis, Jonathan Schropfer, and Kenji Brooks. I also would like to recognize more than 350 volunteers that we have that are our force multiplier when things get tough: the members of the Community Emergency Response Team, our K-9 Search Team, our Radio Amateur Civil Emergency Services Amateur Radio Group, and our Local Emergency Planning Committee. Their professionalism and dedication to strengthen their community resilience is inspirational. They stand ready to provide resources and support when the disasters do occur. I also would like to thank our first responders, healthcare partners, school districts, faith-based organizations, VOADs (Voluntary Organizations Active in Disaster), and our public and private partners for their commitment to collaborate before disasters occur and work together when they do happen. Together, we are all building a more resilient community for today and tomorrow.”
VISUAL PRESENTATION
Ms. Stimson
continued, “I wanted to extend an invite to you and the public to an open house that we’re going to have at the Emergency Operations Center, 714 N. Main, Tuesday, August 18th from 3:00 p.m. to 8:00 p.m. We’re going to have several of our volunteers representing all the different teams that are going to be out there to talk with folks. We’re going to have some displays, some things to kind of give away to help people get prepared, get their individual houses ready to go. You’ll be able to learn how to stay informed of threats, and then how you can get involved in your neighborhood and community’s preparedness. So, then we’ll also give you a tour of our Ready State Emergency Operations Center, which is usually pretty closed off to the public, but we’re going to open it on the 18th from 3:00 p.m. to 8:00 p.m., invite folks to come in, and take a look, and see what we got. Again, I just want to thank you for this proclamation and your continued support. We are honored to serve Sedgwick County residents every day.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Well, thank you very much for all you do. Commissioner Howell.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Thank you, Chairman. I just wanted to make a couple more comments. I want to recognize Bryan especially, because he was like the superman of the year apparently. He was one of the recognized Sedgwick County employees of the year through a recognition program that we have, and so he’s got a trophy cabinet that’s getting bigger all the time. Thank you to Bryan and really your whole team. Julie, I was going to ask you, what kind of emergencies do you actually plan for?”
Ms. Stimson
said, “Everything.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Everything.”
Ms. Stimson
said, “Pretty much.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Like a plane crash? Would a plane crash be one of those?”
Ms. Stimson
said, “Yes. Plane crash, absolutely. In fact, we have a full-scale exercise with the airport in September with a lot of our first responders. It’ll be a full-scale exercise. There’ll be more news about that, but we’re going to light a fuselage on fire, and we’re going to treat it like a plane crash.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “My goodness. Well, we, you know, probably think about floods, and earthquakes, and tornadoes. That’s kind of things that people around the country deal with. There may be a few others that we don’t want to talk about, things like trains, and water supply, and all kinds of other things. So, I did mention it in my previous comments, this is a core function of government. This is one of those things where people say, I have no idea what County Commissioners do, or what county government does, and we say there’s always like 46 different agencies. This is one of those things that we don’t talk about very much, but boy is it important, because when that bad day comes, we’ve got a team of people who they practice every single day what’s going to happen. If this happens, what’s our plan? How are we going to? They practice it, and they practice it, and they practice it. They’ve got everything set up, ready to go. They’ve got equipment. They’ve got written plans. So really, what they do protects all of us. I just want to say, appreciate what you do because, again, even our cities don’t have to think about it because it’s taken care of. So all of us, you know, enjoy, if you will, the comfort, knowing that someone out there has got our back, and it’s you guys. So thank you for what you do. That’s all I want to say.”
Ms. Stimson
said, “Yeah, appreciate it. Thank you.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Thank you very much, Julie. If you don’t mind hanging around here, we’ll try to get a picture here in a few minutes.”
Ms. Stimson
said, “Okay, sure.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Congratulations. Okay, next item, please.”
F.
PROCLAMATION DECLARING NATIONAL HEALTH CENTER WEEK.
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. This will be read by Commissioner Wise.”
Commissioner Wise
said, “Okay. Thank you, Chairman. I am happy to read the proclamation of Sedgwick County:
PROCLAMATION
WHEREAS,
for
over 60 years, Community Health Centers have provided high-quality, affordable, comprehensive primary and preventive health care in our nation’s communities with limited access, delivering value to, and having a significant impact on America’s health care system; and
WHEREAS, as the country’s largest primary care network, Community Health Centers are the health care home for 52 million Americans in over 17,000 communities across the country. One in every seven people in the United States gets their care at a Community Health Center; and
WHEREAS, Community Health Centers develop new approaches to integrating a wide range of services beyond primary care, including nutrition, oral health, vision, behavioral health, and pharmacy services, to provide comprehensive care that allows each patient to thrive. Community Health Centers have more than doubled their behavioral health workforce in the last decade to meet Americans’ growing need; and
WHEREAS, Community Health Centers are locally owned and operated small businesses that serve as critical economic engines, helping to power local communities by generating $261.7 billion in economic activity and supporting more than 809,000 direct and indirect jobs nationally.
NOW, THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED that I, Chairman Jeff Blubaugh, and the Board of Sedgwick County Commissioners, do hereby recognize August 2-8, 2026, as
“NATIONAL HEALTH CENTER WEEK”
and recognize Hunter Health, GraceMed and Healthcore, the three Federally Qualified Health Centers in Sedgwick County, for their vital role in shaping the past, present and future of America’s health care system.

, Chief Executive Officer of Hunter Health, greeted the Commissioners and said, “We’re also joined by Nancy Duling with GraceMed.”

said, “Good morning, Nancy.”
Ms. Feimer
said, “On behalf of Community Health Centers, I want to thank Chairman Blubaugh and the Sedgwick County Board of County Commissioners (BOCC) for this proclamation during National Health Center Week. Since the nation’s first Community Health Center was founded in 1965, Community Health Centers have understood that where we live, work, worship, and play impacts our health. With roots in the civil rights movement, the visionaries who founded the earliest Community Health Centers believed that we could fight poverty and empower communities with health and opportunity.”
Ms. Nancy Duling
, Director of Development at GraceMed Health Clinic, greeted the Commissioners and said, “Today, Community Health Centers play a vital role in enhancing access to essential primary care nationwide. We increase access to crucial primary care by reducing barriers such as cost, lack of insurance, distance, and language. In Sedgwick County, the Federally-Qualified Health Centers (FQHCs) have been instrumental in serving over 70,000 individuals annually, providing a comprehensive array of services, including primary care, dental, behavioral health, vision, and pharmacy services. Every August, we seek to increase the awareness of the nearly 1,500 Community Health Centers nationwide.”
Ms. Teresa Lovelady
, Chief Executive Officer and President of HealthCore Clinic, greeted the Commissioners and said, “All right. This year, the theme of our National Health Center Week is building innovative care where it matters most. It matters most in our communities here in Wichita, here in Sedgwick County. Health is wealth. Health is wealth. Our Community Health Centers, and I’d like to add in Guadalupe Clinic is one of them that gates our safety net, we’re here to be a beacon of hope, strength, and service to care for the most vulnerable people in our communities regardless of their ability to pay, regardless if they have an insurance card. As we celebrate our employees, our board members, and supporters, we want to extend our heartfelt appreciation to Chairman Blubaugh and the Sedgwick County Board of Commissioners for their recognition and support of the significant contributions made by Community Health Centers in Sedgwick County. So, we are so appreciative and thankful to all of you for supporting the work that we do for each person in our community.”

W.
PRESENTATION OF THE QUARTERLY FINANCIAL REPORT.
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Good morning, Brent.”
Mr. Brent Shelton,
Deputy Chief Financial Officer, greeted the Commissioners and said, “This morning I’m bringing you the Quarterly Financial Report for the second quarter, excuse me, of 2026. As we’ll compare our fiscal results against the same time period, the first six months of the year versus 2025. As you know, this report and this presentation have been prepared by the members of the Budget and Accounting teams, and to them I owe a debt of gratitude for putting this report together. This is based on unaudited financial statements. It’s subject to some minor adjustments that you might see as we go through our audit at the end of the year. It has been made, you’ve been given a copy, and this will be made public on the website at the conclusion of today’s meeting.”
VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Shelton
continued, “For the presentation today, because I think we have a couple of other items on the agenda that may take some time, we’ve condensed the report a little bit. I’m going to highlight things that might not be, look like they’re consistent with results, but all in all, we are seeing things that are expected and anticipated. There’s no red flags in our fiscal report. To begin with, let’s look at our anticipated fund balances at the end of the year. The one that I would point out to is based on a trend: our health insurance premiums and costs for employees are tending to go up. So we’re anticipating a deficit in year-over-year or a reduction in fund balance of about $6,000,000 in the Enterprise and Internal Service funds. Beyond that, everything is pretty much anticipated as we go through the course of the year.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “As we break things down into those funds for which we have a dedicated property tax, we look at those five funds, and across those, we’ve been looking at revenues. You’ll see across the bottom a graph that shows revenues over expenditures for the first six months. Those will get closer as we go through the year as the expenditures catch up with revenues that have been received. One thing that I would point out is that ad valorem tax has increased our collections there by about 3.6 percent so far this year, but that’s with an 8.5 percent increase in our assessed value. Your actions to reduce the mill levy last year by a little over a mill have put a cap on those property tax revenues to the county. Otherwise, we’re seeing pretty stable, pretty predictable revenues in those funds. Because personnel expenditures are the largest expense there, we take a look at those, and one thing that we might beginning to see is a bit of a trend: we have an increase in overtime. We haven’t seen that in the past because of staffing stability due to the compensation enhancements that you have approved. In the detention facility, both the adult detention facility and in the juvenile detention facility, we’re seeing a little bit more overtime I think due to a little bit limited staffing reductions, as well as some increased demand for those services. So it’s not alarming at this point, but it’s just something that we’re keeping our eye on.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “Non-personnel expenditures in those funds, you see a couple of large movements on the top two lines. Transfers out were increased as a result of the settlement payment that was made in the first quarter. Then we didn’t make, we closed out our grant award to Exploration Place last year, so that accounts for that decrease in the second row that you see there in grant awards. Let’s take a quick look at the Fire District General Fund. Again, you see that graph on the bottom that shows revenues over expenditures. Again, that follows the same pattern as the county, and we’re seeing pretty stable, pretty predictable increases, revenue of about 8.3 percent and expenditures increasing by about 6.5 percent. So everything continues as projected in the Fire District.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “Now we do have a number of funds for which there is not a dedicated property tax. These are mostly in our human services area, and I would point out that the anomalies as we see movements from year-over-year are primarily as we look at the state revenue in KDADS (Kansas Department for Aging and Disability Services). We received some additional funding there for the state mental health hospital state funding. We’re also seeing, on all other revenue, last year, we got ARPA (American Rescue Plan Act) funds for that same project. We won’t be receiving those this year. So this is all anticipated as we go through the year with these. Some of the key expenditures in those funds, the thing that I would call your attention to in the facilities construction line item, you see a big decrease. Last year, we had increase in revenues for the state mental health hospital. This represents a decrease there and those same expenditures as we compare year-to-year, revenues and expenditures higher last year than this year.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “This just represents what looks like an erratic chart, but it’s our fund balance by quarter over the last five years. You can see that we’re in the second quarter, which is historically the highest quarter for fund balance because we’ve received our property tax distributions. We’ll be spending those down as we go throughout the course of the year. It’s been kind of fun to talk about our investment income because it has been up over the last few years. On a portfolio of $526,000,000, compared to $488,000,000 last year, we earned in the quarter $6,300,000. That’s versus $6,000,000 at the same quarter last year. So investment income from the year to date is up by 11 percent on a year-over-year basis. We’re keeping pace with our benchmark. We’re just a little bit behind it right now because of some interest rate increases that we’ve seen in the marketplace.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “Let’s take a quick look at some capital improvement projects that are ongoing or have seen some significant activity. The COMCARE Crisis Expansion Center, that’s going to house the 988 and some other consolidated COMCARE funds. They’re doing a lot of interior work now, mechanical, plumbing, rough ends, and so forth. That is scheduled to complete in March of next year. The Main Courthouse annex remodel for some Sheriff and Department of Corrections functions is underway, making good progress there. It is scheduled to complete in December of 2026. Couple of bridge projects: this is on 87th Street between Seneca and Broadway. It was completed in June of this year, a brand new bridge in that area. Then finally we have a special assessment project for Brinley Estates: road, sewer, paving, support services for about 25 home sites that’ll be a couple acres on average in size.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “As we look forward, I would leave you with these key points. One is that we’re in sound shape. We’re in good fiscal condition, in good shape to meet the challenges ahead. There’s still a lot of instability in the world that can affect things and does affect our pricing sometimes. The state legislature is looking at a lot of changes that have direct impacts to the county. We’ve seen market volatility, some inflation. It has specifically impacted fuel prices, and that is causing upward pressure on our expenditures. As you move into the budget adoption, there’s kind of a balance here between those increasing costs for fuel, the increasing service expectations and demand, and balancing all that against your goal to continue to reduce the reliance on property tax and provide that relief to the taxpayers of Sedgwick County. Of note is some of those public safety, some of the increasing demands there that we’re just starting to see, that might be something to take a look at. Then continuing to be a competitive employer and take care of our employees because they’re delivering the goods every day to the citizens of this county. With that, I will stand for questions.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Before I open up to any questions, I want to ask: how many of these quarterly reports have you presented to Sedgwick County, Brent? Do you have any idea?”
Mr. Shelton
said, “Over 20.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. How many more are you going to present?”
Mr. Shelton
said, “This is the last one, and I was amazed that so many people turned out to hear my final quarterly report.”

said, “Okay. Scott, if you don’t mind, before we get into this presentation, I want to read a couple notes to everybody in the audience, if you guys will bear with me here. VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Wadle

Predominantly, most of the site would be used for the solar energy conversion system, and then there is a portion that would be used for the battery energy storage system located in the eastern kind of south portion of it. We’ve got a better picture of it in just a moment. Again, those are general boundaries. The specific ones are shown here on the graphic. The site is, as I indicated, 1,490 acres in size. It consists of multiple ownership parcels. The majority of them are zoned Rural Residential. Others are zoned SF-20, Single-Family, and LC, Limited Commercial. The majority of the land within the project area is in agricultural use.”

The MAPC heard comments from the applicants and 44 speakers with 10 speakers in favor of the request and 34 speakers against the request. The MAPC closed the public hearing and voted to defer the application to their June 11th meeting with the request that Planning staff provide follow-up information on multiple items, which are described in the staff report and the attachments. On June 11th, the MAPC considered this application for the second time and the follow-up information that they had requested from the Planning Department. No additional public comment was received since the public hearing was closed at the prior MAPC meeting.”
Mr. Wadle
continued, “The MAPC voted to recommend approval of the application subject to staff recommended conditions, and that vote was seven to five. The number of recommended conditions is 41. I just want to highlight those and will stand for questions if there are specifics about those. The application, now, in terms of protests, the application had initially received 53 protest petitions. Since the submission of the agenda packet for this item, the protest map has been updated in the following ways. First, a protest petition that was initially deemed invalid was determined to be a valid protest, so that was added to the protest percentage, and I’ll show you a map that illustrates this. Then multiple property owners, I believe it was somewhere around the ballpark of 10 property owners, submitted official requests to rescind their protest petitions, and that occurred by the deadline, which was yesterday at 5:00 p.m.”
Mr. Wadle
continued, “The total revised and updated protest percentage is 42.64 percent of the protest area, so this exceeds the 20 percent threshold set by state statute. Therefore, any vote to approve the Conditional Use will require a supermajority or four of five votes. In terms of recommended action, it is recommended that the County Commission adopt the findings of the Planning Commission, approve the Conditional Use request with the recommended conditions, authorize the Chairman to sign the resolution, and authorize the resolution to be published. Again, that requires four or five votes because of the protest. Alternatives include the following: you can override the Planning Commission, adopt alternative findings, deny the application. That would require a two-thirds majority or four out of five votes. Another option is that you could return the case to the MAPC for further consideration with a statement specifying the basis for the failure to approve or deny the application, and that would require just a simple majority or three of five votes. Now, a quick note about staffing: a number of other county staff are here today to help answer questions. In addition, Brent with the Finance Department is here to share insights related to taxes. Likewise, it also requires that all of the project area would be located in or on no more, okay, that’s correct, shall be located in or on no more than six contiguous sections of land. Part of the concern there when the discussion about the regulations was going on was about these kind of spreading out all over the place. The second thing that is related to that is that there’s a requirement that none of the solar projects shall be nearer than two miles to any other approved SECS, Solar Energy Conversion System. So it could come into play, especially with two applications. Then the other one is that no portion of a solar project can be located more than one mile from the balance of the project. Again, that was an attempt to kind of keep these things from spreading out as one project.”

Mr. Shelton
said, “Before I speak to some of the fiscal impacts to local governments, I just want to follow up with one of the ways that we have done financial guarantees for developers that we had advanced special assessment funds to was one of three options: cash, an irrevocable letter of credit, or a surety bond. Those are all in favor of us. So that gives us the guarantee that should something happen, that irrevocable letter of credit is renewed annually. If it’s not renewed, we call it. We bring the money in. So there are ways to go about doing that, just setting the amount. So I know there are a lot of things to consider as you look at this application. So I’m going to just limit the comments here to the fiscal impacts to local governments. I wish I could give you some better numbers than I’m going to be able to, but there’s some lack of guidance, and I mean this is new stuff, new uncharted territory. So we’ll just take a look at both components of this project: the solar piece, the battery piece. We’ll look at the developer’s projections and what they might be given some different assumptions.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “So to begin with, the project calls for about a $310,000,000 investment in solar. Now, Kansas Statutes 79-201 provide for a 10-year property tax exemption on solar electric generating facilities. During the time of that exemption, the value or the cost new of that solar equipment is depreciated down over a 7-year period to 20 percent of its costs new. Then when it goes on the tax roll, it’s taken times the assessment rate, which is 25 percent. The easy way to figure it is a $100,000 investment in solar equipment is a $5,000 assessed value, 20 percent times 25 percent.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay.”
Mr. Shelton
said, “So because of that depreciation, when it goes on the tax roll in year 11, it stays at that level. That’s about the only thing that’s clear cut in some of the rules. The other piece of this is that the underlying real estate that’s currently subject to taxation as ag (agriculture) use generates a nominal amount of tax revenue to the county. The guidelines from Property Valuation Division (PVD) of the Department of Revenue to the County Appraiser when they’re looking at this is that that property needs to be reclassified from ag to commercial because the use has changed. We also have an opinion from Property Valuation Division. It’s not a directive. It’s not guidance. It’s an opinion of someone from Property Valuation Division that the real estate is part of the electric generating part of the project that that would be exempt in that first 10 years. So there’s some uncertainty as to whether we lose the few $1,000 that we’re getting today for the first 10 years or not.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “Then, how do we value that real estate on an ongoing basis in year 11? A simple reclassification from ag to commercial will typically double or triple your revenue stream, the public revenue stream, from that property. However, these are unique in that they have leases, and so there’s some suggestion that an income approach would be used to determine the value of that underlying real estate. So those taxes going forward in years 11 through 30 or 35 are going to be paid by the property owner if they’re continuing to hold title to the property and are leasing it to the solar operator. Now, so that’s some of the nuance around the solar piece of this.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “Let’s take a look then at the battery storage component, and it’s a little less clear. Okay. So around the battery storage component, it could potentially be taxable. It could potentially be partially exempt. It could potentially be fully exempt. It depends upon the definition and whether it’s used to store power, generate power, is attached to the solar project, or is a standalone project on its own. So what we might see there if it’s part of a renewable generation project, that it could fall under that 10-year exemption with the solar project. If it’s a standalone storage system, they could apply for an exemption under the Commercial Industrial Machinery and Exemption (CIME) statutes and have the thing fully exempt for the entire life of the project, or if neither of those happen, then it could be taxable. It’s just going to be a case-by-case basis that’s going to look at the use, and the definition, and the ownership of that property.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay, can I ask you a question here? Okay, I’m back on the, you know, you’re saying 5 percent of 100,000, and I’m back on the $310,000,000 investment. Just trying to ballpark it here, so $15,500,000 in tax savings, is that per year or over the life of the project?”
Mr. Shelton
said, “So that would be about $15,500,000 would be the assessed value that would go on the tax roll. You’re going to take that times the mill levy. If we assume a mill levy somewhere around 120 mills, I think that’s about $1,700,000 a year in revenues from that project.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay, but we don’t really have a hard number as to what we think it going from ag to commercial, what the increase would be there, right?”
Mr. Shelton
said, “Worst case is it goes from what it is today to $0 for 10 years, and then it goes up after that. If this is $10,000 a year now, it could go to $20,000 or $30,000. It could go to significantly more based on the income from the leases.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay, but the first 10 years are probably an estimated at least $1,700,000 or something like that.”
Mr. Shelton
said, “[inaudible]. Yeah.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay, Commissioner Baty.”
Commissioner Baty

Have you been able to talk to other counties? We’re obviously not the first county in the state that has entertained this type of a use. What are you hearing from other County Appraisers of how they’re handling this, and how they’re interpreting the information coming from PVD and the Kansas Department of Revenue?”
Mr. Shelton
said, “I know that our Appraiser has reached out to all of the other County Appraisers and reached out to PVD, and I think at this point she’s not able to get real clarity in terms of what’s uniform across the state.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “Okay.”

said, “It’s just too new to have established some history to see what’s happening.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “Okay. The weight of that on a zoning case, it’s in its own compartment. I understand that. We have property owners that have a financial interest here. It’s not just government that has a financial curiosity of what’s happening here, and we need to keep that in mind as well. That just because the government may not be making and the community may not be making a significant windfall of property tax revenue, there are economic, individuals in this case, that it is a separate economic conversation. So I think that’s important to acknowledge that what I won’t do is say, well, if the government isn’t making a lot of money, nobody else can either. I think that’s bad policy, and I think that’s a bad approach. I will also say that that is incredibly frustrating to not have clear guidance from the state of what these are. These are significant projects. These are significant programs. They pass a tax exemption from the state, not from local government, from the state without clear guidance. To my partners at the state and many friends in the state legislature, they have put local government in a precarious situation because we don’t have clarity of what it is we’re working with. Now we’ll hear from the applicant shortly, and they have an estimate of what they were projecting over a 30-year span. Is it clear to say, you’ve seen this document?”
Mr. Shelton

Battery storage component, if they would apply for an exemption and get a 10-year exemption, I think we’re dropping that $127,000,000 down to $109,000,000. If it’s fully exempt, it’s going to pull $50,000,000 off of the project.”
Commissioner Baty

Mr. Shelton

Commissioner Baty

Mr. Shelton
said, “Yeah.”

Mr. Shelton
said, “The only other piece that I would add to this is that if you have competing projects, if you had a commercial project, or if you had a residential development, the same level investment in those other two will outpace the public revenue stream from a solar just because of that depreciation on, the first 10-year exemption plus the depreciated amount. You compare that to another commercial investment, $100,000,000 in commercial investment is going to go on the roll at $25,000,000. This $100,000,000 goes on at $5,000,000. So, even with a IRB (Internal Revenue Bond) and a property tax exemption for the commercial, because it’s real estate, it goes on and has some appreciation when it’s on the roll. This is a flat line number that goes forward. So it’s better than, in terms of revenue stream to local government, than what it is today. The other two, assuming there’s a competing investment, will outstrip it and ag depending or residential just depending on build out and so forth, and all those are variables that. It’s a ton to review. It’s a ton of information. I’ll explain a little bit why that’s there. Also, apologies for speaking so fast. I only got 10 minutes. We got a whole lot to cover.”
VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Frazier
continued, “Starting with kind of the basics of the project for people that aren’t familiar, even though I imagine most people are. County did a great job instructing earlier on this, but it’s a 260 megawatt project with 260 megawatt battery storage. The facility area is 1,497. The project area is a shade below that, based on the new calculations we had to do for additional information requests. The fenced area is about 1,100 feet. You can see in the same map that the county showed earlier kind of where that goes and also, importantly, setbacks there. We set back from wetlands, transmission lines, utility pipelines, and other things that are really important to be able to avoid, as well as required by law.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “We have 14 landowners working with us in connection with this project. We’re tying into the Murray Gill Substation that’s immediately to the east of the project. I’ll explain a little bit why we’re there in a bit. Also we’re going to be starting construction on this in the first quarter of 2028 if everything goes as planned, and the project should be operational in 2030. As everybody here knows or as a lot have known, especially the Commissioners that were here helping draft the ordinances that we are applying under for our Conditional Use permit, there is an extensive amount of diligence that we had to do to be able to even get to this point. I’m not going to go through each and every one of these items, but I wanted to flag some important ones. First, consultations: we’ve met with basically every environmental department that the local groups have that are relevant to this application as well as local county groups to have a review or consultation that is now included in the application. We have an extensive number of studies and plans that you can see in the bottom left, as well as a list of plans that are going to be reviewed and approved prior to construction by the various entities that Scott noted previously.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “One of the main questions that we get that I wanted to kind of circle up on now that we have an understanding of the project is why we’re here. I’ll spend a little bit of time on each of these, but starting with kind of energy reliability and need. It should be no surprise to anybody here that we are in desperate need of power. SPP (Southwest Power Pool), particularly as we’re facing the extreme temperatures that we have this summer, has been strained on the grid, and there’s been an emergency order that was issued for 17 states, including Kansas, by the federal government recently. Also, the Kansas Corporation Commission also notes that there is a deficit in power availability looking forward in 2028, 2030. I have graphs that we can get to if there’s any further questions about that, but suffice to say, we need power, and we’re a big power generator. The reason that we’re here specifically tying to that substation is because it’s easier, cheaper, and better for ratepayers to be able to tie into existing infrastructure than be able to do line taps.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “I know a lot of people here might be bringing up or would prefer to have these projects on rooftops, contaminated land, other unique places. However, the problem is with economies of scale, you cannot get the single amount of generation that’s valuable to utilities to use without having that existing infrastructure and having a sizable enough project to generate the amount of energy that we are. Lastly, and this is probably the most basic, we need available land. We are a private company working with private landowners. We are not a utility. We do not have eminent domain, and we are not able to bypass zoning regulations, which is another reason we’re here today. If we do not have land, if we do not have willing landowners, if we do not have partners in this, we do not have a project.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “The project location, at least as we believe and I think is demonstrated by all the evidence that you see here, makes a lot of sense for this project. The first and foremost is that it’s a rural area with very few nearby homes. We do projects all over the country and even in the most rural areas, you find it very, very hard to get numbers that are as low as these for surrounding landowners within 1,000 feet of the project. Our number is a little bit more ambitious than the county’s number of 22 that you can see on the back of the handouts. The reason being that we counted basically every single garage, shed, anything that could potentially be a dwelling as dwelling, and that number goes up a little bit higher from that. County calculated 22 people. There’s also seven landowners that are immediately adjacent to the project. Of those seven landowners, two of them are either supportive of the project or are participating, as well as all of the other landowners having the 100-foot setback from property lines and the 250-foot setback from homes. There’s only one project area that has, currently within the site plan, land that has at least one row of panels on all sides, or not all sides, on three sides of it. However, we revised the site plan to remove that area. We would recommend or appreciate codifying that into the condition of approval to make that 100 percent locked in.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “We are in close proximity to sizeable industrial developments, particularly OxyChem, Arkema, Gavilon Grain, Evonik, all of those companies that you see immediately to the east of the project. There’s also existing utility infrastructure, like I mentioned before. We’re a sizeable distance from municipalities, and also importantly, Wichita has been able to cede some of their urban growth area, being that they noted this area has not been slated for future development. There’s no existing or proposed municipal resources out there that would make it attractive for additional residential or commercial development without an extensive amount of new county utility running. That includes water, sewer, new police departments, fire stations, anything like that, within the project area that are proposed other than what is existing there. There’s just currently no water, sewer in that area.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “This is just a little bit more of a rundown of what I mentioned before on the landowners. Again, you could see that the number is a bit higher, and you kind of see stacking up with different agricultural sheds, other things that at least hypothetically could be dwellings, which why our number is higher than what you would see on the county’s report on the back page of those packets. One of the other things I really wanted to highlight here in kind of how we got to this point is a history of the ordinances the county has for solar energy. In 2019, the Board of County Commissioners approved kind of the first set of solar regulations and based on those regulations, we began scouting for solar projects in the area kind of towards the tail end of 2022. For understandable reasons, there was a moratorium passed by the Board of County Commissioners in 2023 that then was designed to inform new ordinances to be able to address a lot of the main concerns I think you’re going to hear later today. That includes concerns about decommissioning, project materials, setbacks, visual screening, adequate zoning, the size of projects, where things can be located. That included public comment, online public survey, discussion from County Association of Cities, Citizen and District Advisory Boards, as well as county staff and Commissioners.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “That was proposed for a vote, went to 13 to 1, and then there was another moratorium following that first initial vote from the MAPC. That led to a fold in of additional consultants, experts to be able to take another look at those ordinances. What you got left with is what we applied under for this last go around. That includes significant setbacks. That includes a cap on projects. That includes a distance threshold from other projects. That includes visual simulations, transportation assessments, landscape plans, all sorts of other plans that lead to us having 1,000-plus page application that I’m sure you’ve at least taken a fair amount of time to look through. It’s definitely not super fun bedtime reading, but it’s all in there. Following that, the Wichita UGA was kind of a main hindrance to our project. There was a condition put in there that the urban growth area cannot be affected by solar projects. That urban growth area was removed unanimously by the Wichita City Council without public comment to allow for our project to come forward. This kind of goes against the lack of development that’s expected for this area. If Wichita expected development to be coming to this area, they would not have done so, and certainly would not have voted unanimously without comment to do so.”
Mr. Fraizer
continued, “We also had, as was noted by Scott previously, a MAPC hearing that was initially deferred on the 29th. That led to the tax model review, water use information, and other clarifications that have been folded into the public record as part of those, and then were eventually voted on on 6/11 by the MAPC for project approval, which is what is in front of you today requiring the four out of five votes with the protest to approve. Also, between that last hearing and now, we’ve done outreach to neighboring landowners, which kind of points to the rescission documents and the map that was shown by Scott previously, showing where we’ve been able to make inroads, talk to people, really get ideas about the project out there.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “One of the things that I’m not going to spend as much time, but I was certainly prepared for it, appreciate the Finance Department doing a great job covering this, is taxes. We are similarly frustrated with the lack of guidance that the state has been able to provide for this. We have done as good as we can without having clear codified rules that speak to particularly the battery storage facility as well as portions of the solar. However, as referenced previously, we anticipate an up to $129,000,000 tax revenue over the life of the project for this. We also laid that out by tax revenue by year, and this kind of factors in the depreciation schedule. You’re going to be seeing those numbers go down as the battery storage piece depreciates for kind of the first 10 years of that life.”
Mr. Fraizer
continued, “You’ll see a jump as the solar component comes out, and then kind of a more flat revenue as everything depreciates based on its established schedules. There’s also some small spikes in the battery storage facility at the beginning as we augment and replace battery equipment to effectively make it new again. In short, again, there’s $129,000,000 for 35 years of its life, referencing and including in that model all of the abatements that were referenced by the Finance Department previously. However, we do believe that the battery storage facility will be taxed, and we are welcome to codify that as well to ensure that that is done so without the guidance from the state. We are happy to solve that issue, clarify that guidance, and put it in the conditions of approval.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “I won’t cover on the clarifications covered pretty well previously. Another question we get is related to operation. The project will have insurance for complete coverage as required by statute. The water use for it will be dramatically less than is already occurring on irrigated parcels that we’re retiring for it, about less than one acre-foot of water annually and up to eight acre-feet of water for construction dust management. Also, noise has been modeled. The battery storage facility is extremely far away from homes, upwards of 2,000 feet as well as the inverters being centrally located and not being audible for any surrounding homes. The battery storage facility, again, I won’t touch on, but suffice to say, there are extensive conditions of approval that you can find before you in a motion to approve or within the existing conditions that speak directly to fire safety as well as future coordination and fire plans to follow. We will be inspecting all of the panels, but using industry standard panels that do not have any contamination risk, and this has been reported and reviewed by the Environmental Department, as well as something that they’ll do in the future.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “In decommissioning, we expect to be able to recycle or reuse basically all of the equipment that’s part of this as well as having financial assurance to cover the county in the event that the project is abandoned. Lastly, quick 10 minutes, we believe this project makes sense for the county. We believe it makes sense for our landowners. We believe that we’re turning land that is currently not being used or developed to its full extent into one of the largest tax revenue generators for the county, as well as benefiting our landowners and local workers, which you’ll hear about later. Thank you.”

That’s what we insure it for, and that’s what the contract that we would have with the utility for the battery is. However, looking forward, there’s really no reason why that wouldn’t continue out into the future. We just wanted to be conservative in our estimates because, you know, kind of not wanting to overpromise here. In the idea of repowering, there’s really no reason to believe that the battery storage facility would not continue on. It’s just not included in this model for the reasons I mentioned.”

VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Frazier
continued, “So at least in the operational part, and I can kind of cut to this on the operation piece, we’re expecting annually for both of those washings, not, you know, one, you know, adding them together, but being under one acre-foot of water, so about 220,000 gallons. There’s really not as water intensive as you might imagine to clean the panels because a lot of is being brushed and not kind of just blindly spraying everything with a hose. What you’re going to see for kind of larger water use here is going to be construction dust mitigation. The idea there being is we’re moving kind of larger trucks on gravel roads that we will be paying for any of the damages for under the road use agreement referenced previously.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “We’ll be wanting to mitigate dust for surrounding homes and homeowners as well as agricultural fields. We anticipate roughly eight acre-feet of water being used for that dust control management portion, which, compared to, I mean, really just kind of any agricultural use, is markedly less than you would see. I believe the state of Kansas actually estimates two acre-feet of water as reasonable use for one acre of agriculture. So a small, small portion of that, as well as we’re retiring some irrigated parcels over the life of the project. So it should be a net water gain for the area instead of a net water use, given the retirement of some of those irrigated parcels that are out there already.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Okay, so then the water that’s being utilized, is that from the ground, or is that municipal water? Where does it come from?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “So non-municipal water, usually how this works is if we have a well on site that’s rated, and most of these ones because they would be agricultural wells would be rated to be able to get us the water quantity that we need. We’d obviously be paying for that water. Nothing in this life is given for free. However, what we see in a lot of other situations is kind of trucked water to the site, like you might see at other construction sites with kind of water tanker trucks coming in. Again, that would be water that we would be purchasing, wouldn’t need to be tying into municipal funds for that. Likely going through existing wells with agricultural fields that we have, that I referenced before, or that trucked water that we’d be paying for and brought on site.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “So with agricultural fields, those water rights continue to your usage?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Correct.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Okay, I did not know that. In regards to mowing, the vegetation, and the use of herbicides, and that type of thing, can you please elaborate a little bit on the plan? What are you planning on doing to keep the vegetation down?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, so there’s a couple things that tie into that. The first one being that we’re kind of converting all of that land back to native prairie and native pollinators. It’s one of the benefits that we actually see of being able to turn over some of that land, get some of those nutrients back in. So everything within that fenced area that’d be covered with panels, that would not essentially be just the battery facility and the substation, which is a small, small, small fraction of the acreage, is going to be reseeded with native pollinators. One of the benefits of that is they don’t grow as high in a lot of circumstances, but definitely will need to be maintained periodically. We envision probably, you know, twice a year to four times a year mowing or other options that we’re exploring would be able to use level of grazing, maybe not across the entire site. If we could find partners for that, it’s kind of a good look for us as a solar company to be able to bring in, you know, sheep populations. Really, goats or other things don’t work because they get on the panels, but sheep. We’re open to it. We don’t have any contracts or anything signed like that, but would have, again, kind of two to four times a year mowing to maintain that and keep the land or I guess the plants from growing over the solar panels and eating into efficiency.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “I’ll probably ask Scott later, or one of our other staff, but in terms of the regulation of, again, the management of the vegetation. I guess I’ll ask some questions later on that. In regards to fire mitigation, I don’t know if solar panels can burn. I don’t know how that works, but certainly the battery storage is a concern. Please talk about can the fire trucks go between the panels or between the rows? Are they wide enough for fire trucks if they had to put a fire out? Can they get down the rows and that type of thing? Then with the battery storage, what’s the technology planned to be used for the battery storage?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, so I’ll kind of start with the battery piece because I think that’s the most pertinent to this, and then I’ll get into the solar rows. So I pulled up here while you were talking about the proposed conditions of approval that are included in the battery storage facility. This is on top of kind of the existing requirements and honestly restates a lot of what we’re expecting to do.”
VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Frazier
continued, “So in addition, and I’m not going to go through each and every one of them, but in addition to the pretty sizable amount of existing fire codes, testing standards, etc. for the systems, we’ll also be using a lithium iron phosphate or LFP battery chemistry. It’s a little bit different from antiquated systems, and the reason being is that the older systems and battery chemistries used what’s called nickel manganese cobalt or NMC. The main change for those is that even though the NMC batteries were technically more energy dense, you know, from an economics perspective, you could get more power in smaller area, you were having heavy metals like nickel, manganese, and cobalt, which meant if you had any sort of emergency at those facilities, you would be getting a lot more toxic materials into both the air, soil, and groundwater. You essentially eliminate any of those risks with the lithium iron phosphate battery cells that we would be using for this, mostly because those heavy metals are not included.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “The battery storage facility, again, I won’t touch on, but suffice to say, there are extensive conditions of approval that you can find before you in a motion to approve or within the existing conditions that speak directly to fire safety as well as future coordination and fire plans to follow. We will be inspecting all of the panels, but using industry standard panels that do not have any contamination risk, and this has been reported and reviewed by the Environmental Department, as well as something that they’ll do in the future. A report that I cite often was done by the, I believe it’s Fire Research Association or FRA was the name of the entity, they looked at 35 battery fire fires at American facilities between 2012 and 2024, looking at exactly this question of if we have an existing, you know, fire event, emergency event at one of these facilities, what happens to the air, soil, and groundwater both at the facility while the fire is going on, and then immediately after, and some time following that event. In all of those instances, all 35, there was no evidence of either soil, groundwater, or air contamination that would rise to a hazardous level in any sort of fire event.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “The only facility that, at least I’m aware of in America, that’s had any kind of contamination concern is an event called Moss Landing, using a completely different kind of battery technology, using those NMC batteries as well as kind of a warehouse enclosure. That is why we’re using a sort of modular approach. I can elaborate a lot on this, but the basics of it is that if you have 260 megawatts of battery storage, it’s a lot safer if you have 260 1-megawatt containers to fold down that risk than having 260 megawatts in one single building. The last thing I’ll say on this before I talk about the fire piece is that we’ll be coordinating directly with Fire District Number One. That is the responding entity in this. It’s both codified in the conditions of approval already, and it’s something that we do in all of our projects, even in places like Texas where we don’t have to go through these zoning processes and get the same permits. So we’ll be doing training with them prior to construction, prior to commissioning of the project, and then it’s required by code to happen every single year thereafter.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “The same will apply to the solar portion of the project. Essentially, we’ll probably be doing all of this as a bundle. We’ll be coordinating with them for a whole facility to be able to address concerns within the field. I think, as it relates to fires in the solar portion of the project, what we see typically as major impacts is kind of fires coming into solar facilities as opposed to fires originating at them. Part of that goes back to the mowing and the pollinator vegetation discussion that I had before. If you’re going to have lower grasses that are more managed, you’re not really going to have the same sort of fire risk that everything folds, and it flows out. There is, you know, of course, with any electrical equipment some issue or at least some concern that a fire could originate. The mowing should resort or restrict that to being kind of isolated pockets that could be immediately addressed. So there’s a perimeter access road. In terms of the panel rows themselves, typically, not usually kind of the rows are, you know, outfitted for kind of smaller equipment that you could load, kind of like a Gator, where you could put individual kind of panels on to replace. There will be a perimeter access road that kind of speaks to the setbacks for this facility, where fire trucks can get inside and kind of around all that.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “They may not, again, to be clear, they may not be able to get the fire trucks between the rows?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Correct.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Okay, and again, you use the term lithium iron phosphate. I’m not familiar with that entire term, but the word lithium battery, I think we all know what that is.”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Right.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Is this the type of fire that basically, if it does catch on fire, you have to basically let it burn out? You can’t. You can contain it, but you’re not going to be able to put it out with any type of chemical response.”
Mr. Frazier
said, “That’s correct. This again speaks to kind of the containerized approach here, where if you had 260 megawatts of batteries going up that you can’t extinguish, and this is what we saw at Moss Landing, you’re having a real big issue. However, based on testing, and that’s the UL (Underwriters Laboratories) 9540A that you see referenced in the condition of approval that all of our enclosures would use, those are tested to ensure setbacks from one container to the next do not allow a fire to jump. So even if we have something, and I won’t go through the ins and outs, but even if something is not caught by the 24/7 monitored battery management system that would automatically move power out of a cell if there’s an issue identified. At worst, you are losing one container or 1/260 of the facility, which is 1/260 of the risk. So, in short, yeah, you would have to let it burn, but the risk is minimal.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Is that something that’s been tested in terms of a real world event, and is there some type of certification that tells us that that’s how this is designed? It meets some type of a protective specification.”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, UL 9540A is a certification process other than a code, and we have seen this in other battery storage facilities where there’s been one container that has, you know, caught on fire or had an issue, whereas the surrounding containers have been unaffected. I will say, as it relates to the water, sorry to keep cutting you off on all of these, you might see water used not on the container that’s burning for, you know, the reasons that you just mentioned. With electrical fires, you’re not trying to put water on them, but in some specific circumstances if there’s a prevailing wind or some reason to do so, the fire department would come out and place water on adjacent containers to just kind of reinforce that cooling. So it’s already established in the UL 9540A certification that a fire is not going to be able to jump. If we’re adding another layer of redundancy on this, that would be in the emergency response plan to be able to apply fire [sic: water] to adjacent containers to further mitigate that risk.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Is there some type of a control room that’s on the property that, again, there’s battery storage, and there’s solar panels, is there some way, some place where you guys go that essentially is a monitoring station that you’ll be able to turn things on and off and that type of thing?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah. So generally speaking, everything works remotely. It works automatically at the first stage, and then an off-site monitoring facility would be able to also manually move the power out, or at least flip the switches and make the control changes. However, for first responders mostly, we have what’s called kind of an emergency terminal that would be on the outside of the fence that would have more or less a laptop that’s integrated with backup power that you would be able to open. It would show every single container in the facility, kind of the exact conditions that’s going on there. So, without even getting into the site, any first responder arriving on scene would be able to have that information in front of them before they, you know, kind of run into the facility.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “I’m only down to a couple more questions at this point, but…”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, fire away.”

The tree fencing is evergreen trees planted at 5 feet that would be reaching maturity somewhere around 10 years. I believe that the way that the landscape plan is set up, that would be about 10 to 20 feet. So the idea being that over the, you know, kind of fiveish years of the life of the project, it’d be screening effectively all of that fencing. Again, something where we understand there’s kind of sensitivities in specific areas, we are willing to add conditions or make modifications to that landscape plan in the future.”

Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah. So the trees would always be on the outside of the fencing, and then based on what the county had recommended, which is included in that landscape plan you see, we have trees basically around the entire perimeter of the project, other than the few places where there’s kind of interior fencing next to an agricultural land. So roadways, adjacent to homes, you’re going to see tree, fence pretty much uniformly around the entirety of the project.”
Commissioner Howell
