Commissioner Howell
said, “Okay, and then there was some discussion earlier about a decommissioning bond, and I know there’s another theory that maybe it could be an escrow. Do you have any thoughts about that?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “I’ll say from this side, and this is actually something I wanted to bring up and didn’t have time to in the speech. There’s a condition of approval within the conditions that you can see before you in the motion, 23 specifically, that requires that the bonded amount, the escrow amount, whatever, for the decommissioning be provided 60 days following this approval, far ahead of the start of construction. We would recommend a letter of credit, or most, you know, usually a surety bond, and we have documentation from surety bond providers included within our public comments as well to be able to satisfy that. The other thing that we would recommend would be amending that condition of approval 23 to allow that bonding to be posted nearer to the term of construction because currently as it’s, you know, presented, we’re bonding for something that has no impact.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “Prior to construction, there’s really no work being done on the project. There’s nothing to decommission. There’s nothing to reclaim. The money is more or less something that we’re having to provide without any sort of advancement of the project. So we are open to all three, would strongly prefer a surety bond. It’s how we see this done in pretty much every single other municipality that we work in, not only around Kansas but around the rest of the country, but would recommend that that condition of approval 23 at the very least be tied to a year from today, or at the very, you know, probably at the best prior to the start of construction. Still covers the expectation or the request from the county to be able to have the funds available if, you know, something happens to the project, and there needs to be decommissioned, but doesn’t provide an additional burden that, from our view, doesn’t solve any problems.”
Commissioner Howell

Mr. Frazier

Commissioner Howell

You put a certain amount of money down, and then if the decommissioning is never exercised, then there’s money that comes in maybe from the person that carries that policy, if you will. Whereas the escrow is basically cash up front sitting in a bank somewhere, and you got to put all that money up on the front end. Am I right about saying it that way?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “100 percent correct. Yeah, we worked with Varney. What you described is exactly right.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “I guess my last question right now would be, since you do have a battery storage, let’s talk about any type of sound production, and again attenuation, and propagation. How much? I just want to talk about the issue of sound entirely, so please elaborate.”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, I’ll even do you one better and cover the inverters too because those are kind of the two main noise generators that would be around the project. So the battery storage facility, I think it’s one of the big benefits of our project, is right next to the substation. Importantly, that substation is right next to OxyChem, and also, as you could imagine, a substation is kind of the terminus of a lot of high voltage transmission lines that run north, south, east, and west all over that area. Homes from that battery storage facility are, I think, at a minimum 2,500 feet away, or in excess of 2,000 feet away. So, based on the propagation of that noise, kind of rule of squares, kind of getting further and further away that noise dissipates. We’ve modeled and provided this in our environmental assessment, but do not expect the noise for anybody’s homestead, at least, to be more than ambience. I think tying into that goes to all of the noise generation that’s already occurring in that area from industrial development as high voltage transmission lines that also make that little whir if you’re standing near or under them.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Let me restate what you just said, make sure that you agree.”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yes.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “You’re telling me that if I’m in someone’s home, I can’t hear the sound generated at the inverters or the battery storage? It’s indiscernible, basically.”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Correct. Yeah, the battery storage facility is located far enough away from any homes to be audible, as are the inverters. The inverters make a sizably less amount of noise, so while they are technically closer to homes than the battery storage facility would be, we’ll have centralized inverters for the various rows that are inside the fenced area of the project that are centrally located to keep them far, far away from the homes, and the noise generated from them dissipates in a much lower distance than the battery storage facility does.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “I know this may not be much of a question, but maybe more of a comment for everyone’s, because I asked this question numerous times, and I finally got an answer. Mr. Frazier
said, “Correct.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “How tall are they again?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “So kind of where you would rack a panel is generally kind of top of my head around sixish feet, kind of give or take, based on individual conditions of the, you know, topography, but it’s pretty flat out there for anybody that’s driven that area. So usually mounted about six feet. If you’re looking at kind of up at the taller end, you’re about eight to nine feet if a panel’s, you know, kind of straight up and down. Generally speaking though, a panel would never be straight up and down unless there’s some sort of emergency circumstance, you know, severe weather, a reason to have them arranged that way. Typically, you’re about seven odd feet, kind of moving right to left at the very, very tip of the panel. So, with six-foot screening, as well as the trees in front of it, you know, maybe you see the crest of a panel every now and again at kind of the very early morning or very late at night, but during the middle course of the day, they’ll be flat around, you know, five, six feet under the fence.”
Commissioner Howell
said, “So part B of my question would be this: that is, the panels generate DC (Direct Current), but there’s an inverter on each row or near each row that converts it from DC to AC (Alternating Current). Then it’s transmitted to essentially towards the battery storage area. It may be stored, or maybe most, I guess my opinion is probably would be stored because you probably want to convert that back to sellable electricity at peak demand, if you will. That’s when I would assume that’s most profitable for the company. Anyway, it’s transmitted from the inverter to the battery storage area, where then it may be converted back to DC and stored. At which point later on, whenever it’s consumed, this goes from the batteries back through some type of an inverter that configures it for injection onto the grid. So generally, that’s how this whole system is designed. Am I saying that roughly correct?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yes. The only thing that I would add is that, you know, during kind of peak demand, or if there’s, you know, times where the utility who effectively controls the generation for this under the contracts that we would have. You know, we don’t have the opportunity to kind of flip on and off the battery system. We would have what’s called a tolling agreement with Evergy that they would run when that’s going to be used. So it’s more kind of utility infrastructure. What the, you know, solar facility would also sign is a power purchase agreement or something similar that is paid at a specified percentage and also fed directly to utility. So, in most circumstances under operation, exactly what you said would be correct. We take DC power from the panels, convert to AC at the inverters, run to the battery, you know, convert down to DC, store, convert back up to AC, and provide to the grid. However, there’s certainly situations, and we see this all over the place, where there’s a need for energy now. Maybe even
during the day, there’s some sort of a unique demand. The DC from the, in those situations, the DC that’s generated can be transmitted to the AC that could then feed and bypass the battery storage facility directly onto the grid. So both are possible. The battery storage piece, kind of the additional conversion, is more likely, but we can also get directly to the grid as well through the substation.”

said, “Okay. Another question: I see a lot of union workers in here eager to get this kind of work, and I’m just curious. How many workers do you anticipate, and what kind of timeline do you see in the area for construction?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, so entire length of construction is about 12 to 18 months. The first, I’d say, three or so months of that is likely going to be site preparation. A lot of the main electrical work probably comes between months 6 to 12 to 18, and kind of about that years’ timeline there. We anticipate at peak construction, and obviously this will waiver based on the demands of the site, roughly 200 construction jobs that are full-time equivalent. So roughly 200 jobs. Obviously, you see all of our fantastic union supporters here today. We anticipate a lot of that labor, if not all of it, to be sourced locally with skilled craftsmen and tradespeople that have experience doing renewable projects, electrical work. That would be running for more or less the 6-month period of that project development to about I’ll call it 16 because the last 2 is more commissioning, and the needs will drop a little bit there, but definitely, we’ll need labor throughout the entirety of that.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay, so probably 200 cars going in there, and I don’t think any of this is paved, so I assume a lot of dust. Is that something that, do you guys do anything to water down the roads? I don’t know that you can even legally do that. I don’t know if you, how have you guys done that in other areas? Do you work with the county to try to get them to water down the roads, or how does that work?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, so we’re responsible for that, and this kind of goes back to those conditions of approval that were mentioned before. We have to do a lot of coordination before we’re even able to move any trucks onto that land with the Public Works department, as well as getting preliminary understandings of haul roads to minimize kind of east-west traffic and make everything more or less north-south. We do expect to do dust mitigation through exactly the ways that you mentioned, mostly watering of the ground to keep the water wet and prevent that dust from kind of emerging. That goes to that eight acre-foot during the course of construction number that I cited previously used just for dust mitigation during that time.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “The other thing that I think is really important to mention that Scott brought up previously is that we’d have a road use agreement with the county. What that road use agreement would require is that prior to construction, we’re taking an inventory of all those roads. What do they look like? What do they need? Are they suitable for haul? Any upgrades to those roads that we need to make to be able to allow for construction, we’ll be paying those fees. That’s not passed on to county taxpayers. In the same sense that any damage to those roads, maintenance as we’re doing construction, then afterwards to get them back to the condition they were in, we are also paying those fees. We are doing that maintenance, or at the very least, we are contracting with the county to do that maintenance. So it’s something we’re certainly considering. It’s included in the conditions of approval, and we’re going to be required for a vote today under the conditions of approval to, you know, do those plans, make that consultation, pay those costs, and fix those roads to be able to be suitable for construction. Then fix them if anything comes up.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. After two years, how many local jobs do you see there?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “So not very many. Once we start operation of the project, we anticipate about two to three jobs being needed. Of course, that would be kind of full-time equivalent monitoring of the system, or kind of supported jobs through either Evergy to monitor the system, or basically kind of upkeep and consistent maintenance. We’d also be contracting. We don’t reflect this in that estimate, but also be contracting with local landscaping companies to do the mowing that I referenced previously, or the grazing that we might do with kind of local agricultural suppliers, as well as, like you alluded to, any of that kind of tree maintenance, watering, pruning, you know, fence fixing, any kind of periphery stuff.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. I don’t know if you mentioned it earlier, but you have some video surveillance or something to watch for fire, or fire alert systems, or...”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, so I’ll talk about it kind of in two pieces because the battery storage facility is a little bit different than the solar portion. So the battery facility itself works where, at this point in the industry and I expect if we’re not already there with every manufacturer, we will be in 2029 with the next fire codes, you can monitor the batteries down to the individual cell level. So that’s kind of one individual battery cell. It’s not a AA battery, but relatively similar to that that then is put in a box that’s called a rack, and then put into kind of a larger cabinet called a module. We can monitor that 24/7 remotely down to that individual cell level. So the idea being that if there’s a fluctuation in temperature, there’s a fluctuation in pressure, really, there’s any kind of abnormal situation happening, we’re going to get the power out of that battery as soon as absolutely possible. The system does that automatically if anything’s tripped.”
Mr. Frazier
continued, “Then also, like I mentioned to Commissioner Howell previously, there’s going to be monitoring, as I referenced with one of those jobs, to be able to kind of hit a button, flip a switch, and get that out if for whatever reason it’s not automatically done. So layers of redundancy there. That’s, generally speaking, how it’s done with that level in addition to kind of the outside the fence monitoring. As it relates to the solar portion, there’s a less advanced but similar type system that monitors kind of individual rows and individual panels. Generally speaking, the reason that’s done is because we need to be able to satisfy a certain level of capacity for a contract with a utility. If we’re not generating up to that level, we get penalized just like anybody else would be for non-compliance with a contract. So if there’s any sort of malfunction at the facility, any sort of degradation or issue with the solar panels, we’re going to know that through that system as well, and that will kick off the emergency response plan that we’ll coordinate with the local responding fire department.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Commissioners, do we have any other questions right now?”
Commissioner Howell
said, “Not now.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay, thank you very much.”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yeah, thank you.”

Mr. Dennis Woods
, 5201 S. 119th St. W., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’m here to speak in opposition to the utility-scale solar application by Mission Clean Energy. I’ll be covering Unified Zoning Code Number Three, more commonly referred to as number three of the Golden Rules, which reads the extent to which removal of the restrictions will detrimentally affect nearby property. Now, before I get into the details, a little history on the Golden Rules. The Golden Rules were developed as a result of a 1978 Supreme Court case, and the justices found in favor of the plaintiffs with specific set of criteria that must be met in a zoning change.”
Mr. Woods
continued, “If enacted, these property owners within 1 mile will lose between 6 and 30 percent of their value. Statistically, you could also see reductions up to three miles from this property. I can’t think of many more detrimental impacts than the loss of someone’s home value, where the majority of folks carry a significant percent of their total net worth in their home’s value. I believe in free market enterprise. I believe in individual property rights.”
Mr. Woods
continued, “Everyone here from the opposition is concerned about their property rights, and it’s a privilege to speak for those that may not feel too comfortable in doing so. If you own the land, you buy it, you pay for it, you do with it what you want, as long as you don’t infringe on the rights of others, meaning the nearby property owners. If we’re not careful here, we’re going to enrich 14 people at the expense of 100s. There’s no greater good in this project, meaning the benefits do not in any way outweigh the cost to the nearby property owners. It doesn’t meet the Golden Rules or Unified Zoning Code. A overwhelming majority of the eligible property owners within 1,000 feet of the project area signed a protest petition, covering almost twice the acres needed for a supermajority protest. Since it doesn’t meet the Unified Zoning Code, I request that you deny this application. Thank you.”

Mr. George Palmer
, 7101 S. 103rd St. W., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’m in opposition to the Galena Solar Project. Today, I will be speaking on Golden Rule Number Five. Golden Rule Number Five: relative gain to the public health, safety, and welfare as compared to the loss in value or hardship imposed upon the applicant. The applicant property owners will not suffer loss in value if application is denied. The land still holds value as agriculture or future development of residential subdivisions. The foreign-held solar company will not suffer hardship. The application states that there is a benefit to the county in both economic and gain of additional electricity. Both of these claims are incorrect. The county will suffer economic losses during the first 10 years. The fact that the majority of the new property in the application is tax exempt puts an enormous burden on health, safety, and welfare. The additional burden on law enforcement and Fire District alone should be enough to deny this application.”
Mr. Palmer
continued, “The application states the county will receive around $2,300,000 per year. This is already a terrible return on investment. Now add in additional cost, police, fire, regulatory permits from the county, additional time spent by county staff to approve and regulate, and today our own county CFO [sic: Deputy CFO] says it’s the third best use of land. The claim that this provides needed additional energy is also false. There is no shortage of electricity in the region. It is likely that this project will send a majority of power, if not all, to other regions. The Energy Information Agency of the federal government states that Kansas is an exporter of electricity. Senator Mike Thompson of the state Senate, who sits on the Utility Committee, states Kansas exports 56 percent of its electricity. The applicant claim of being deficient in electricity is based on the assumptions of data centers’ growth. Guess what? Data centers don’t want solar. Data centers want reliable energy. The reason the Southern Power Pool issued emergency is because solar and wind underperformed, as per their report. Industrial solar is not clean. It’s not cheaper. It’s not reliable. It does not help the grid. I urge the Commission today to deny this application, based on most of the Golden Rules not being met. Thank you.”

VISUAL PRESENTATION
Ms. Martin

Additional images show the access roads used around the proposed facility after a rain in June 2026. , 5627 S. Tyler Rd., greeted the Commissioners and said, “We oppose the Conditional Use permit of the Galena Solar Project. Mr. Kaden Martin
, 9212 W. 47th St. S., greeted the Commissioners and said, “Pretty good. How are you doing?”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Good.”
Mr. Martin
said, “Good. I am here today to speak on behalf of children around the world who are likely younger than me and cannot be here to speak for themselves. While the solar panels used for this project will be assembled in the United States, the parts and pieces will be manufactured in other parts of the world. Manufacturing of solar panels is a very dirty process. It relies on high heat, chemical refinement, and mining. Key pollutants include silicon tetrachloride, toxic heavy metals, and mining waste, which all contribute to air, land, and water pollution in third world countries that have little to no regulations. Even more disturbing than that are the child labor laws in those countries. According to International Labor Organization, there are up to 1,000,000 children working under hazardous mining conditions. There are 12 countries on the list of major exploiters concerning mining of critical minerals like cobalt, copper, lithium, tin, and cadmium. Democratic Republic of the Congo, Zambia, Zimbabwe, and Bolivia are some of the worst offenders, but there are others.”
Mr. Martin
continued, “The greatest documented concern involves polysilicon, the primary material used in the manufacturing of solar panels. Other documented materials are ingots and wafers. According to the Department of Labor and other federal agencies, China uses forced labor and child labor. China produces 45 percent of the world’s solar-grade polysilicon, 98 percent of ingots, 97 percent of wafers, and 77 percent of solar modules. Working conditions for these children are horrible to say the least. They dig tunnels, haul heavy loads, and handle toxic metals with no protection. I think it’s safe to assume you would not want your children or other kids like me working in these conditions. I ask Sedgwick County to take a stand today and say no to the participation in an industry that relies on child labor. For the safety of future generations around the world no matter where they live, I urge you to say no to industrial solar today. Thank you.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Kaden, do you do a lot of public speaking?”
Mr. Martin
said, “No, not really.”

said, “You did a great job today. Would you take a question?”

Ms. Kimberly Palmer
, 7101 S. 103rd St. W., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’m in opposition to the Galena Solar Project. Today, I will be speaking about property rights. This seems to be one of the sticking points concerning industrial-sized solar. The applicants believe that they have the right to do with their land as they wish. This is true up to the point that it interferes with other citizens’ property rights. The groups of citizens I want to defend today is homeowner and landowners, taxpayer and ratepayer. Homeowner and landowners will suffer between 6 and 30 percent loss in property value up to 3 miles. I’m referencing the book that Dr. Seiler submitted earlier.”
Ms. Palmer
continued, “There are four independent studies in this booklet that are quite thorough. Mission Clean states that their studies have been largely inconclusive. I’m curious on why Mission Clean would offer bribes disguised as cooperative agreements to protest petition signers. Why would they need to pay landowners around the project if it did not devalue their property? They are paying because they know it does devaluate their property. So, whose property rights will you defend today? The property rights of the non-applicants, or Kuwait, Denmark, and 14 landowners who will profit at others’ expense? Taxpayers are the next group of citizens whose rights are being violated, specifically property taxpayers. My husband and I owned a business for 25 years. We employed between 8 to 12 persons. We never got a property tax break there or at our residence.”
Ms. Palmer
continued, “Every person or company that pays property tax is paying more so the applicant doesn’t pay any for the first 10 years. By then, the items that are taxable will be depreciated down to 20 percent, which is salvage value. Whose rights will you defend today? Non-applicants or the group I mentioned earlier? Ratepayer is the last group. The rates will increase. Industrial solar is not cheaper. It is most expensive and least reliable form of generation. The cost, even with massive tax subsidies, will be passed onto the citizens of Sedgwick County. There are many references to industrial solar not being cheap or reliable. Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) is where to look. FERC 1A form gives the cost of generation by utility and grid operators. Don’t vote today to raise my electric rates for unreliable energy. It is a simple fact: anyone’s property rights ends when they infringe on somebody else’s. I urge the Commission today to please deny this application.”

Ms. Lori Lawrence
, 321 N. Lorraine Ave., greeted the Commissioners and said, “Thank you, Commissioners, for letting me speak this morning. I wanted to give you some reasons to approve the solar project. Out of respect for your fellow service people in elected office, you should approve it. Number two, this project is free. That’s one of the arguments is that well, why don’t we put it on people’s roofs? Then people have to pay for it. This is not something that we have to pay for. The investors that Mission Clean Energy has will pay the entire cost, including all of the maintenance and everything that is involved with it.”
Ms. Lawrence
continued, “There are no emissions, like there are when you drill for oil or pump methane, and the sun shines all day in peace and quiet. Even when it’s not 100 percent, we’re still making solar power. There’s no noise that anyone is going to be close enough to hear. There’s not going to be any dust once construction’s over with, but farming generates dust every time you have to bite into the soil. So we’ve lost a lot of topsoil in the state already because of traditional farming practices. So I’m relieved to say that many people are now using cover cropping and things that will help with that, but solar panels will not turn the ground at all once they’re put in, and that land will stay there. Look at No-till on the Plains for more information about not tilling your ground. The project also uses very little water compared to, as you’ve heard already, I mean you have these facts. Water conservation is a state priority, and solar uses next to nothing.”
Ms. Lawrence
continued, “There won’t be any chemicals sprayed, hopefully, and you won’t have to fertilize pollinator plants. Pesticides and herbicides pollute groundwater, and we see those every day. Without the fungicides and the other chemicals, the mycorrhizal organisms in the soil will return that are no longer there. The sun will never stop shining. It’s always going to shine. Kansas is the eighth sunniest state in the United States. We’re sunnier than Florida. We need to take advantage of that. Even on a day with clouds, the solar will still produce. Maybe not quite as much, but they will still produce. If there’s battery storage, that’s what it’s there for. When it’s overproduction during the day, that will store that power that will be able to be drawn out at later time. That’s what they’re for. Batteries now, we’re not talking about 20 years ago, are incredibly safe.”

There won’t be any transmission lines that someone’s mentioned before. Solar is temporary. Housing is permanent. This will come up after 30 years, and then there’ll be farmland again. Only it’ll be better farmland because it will not have been plowed all this time. Property taxes on the batteries will start very quickly, and you will start seeing that benefit, y’all. Taxes on the panels will be 10 years, and then they’ll contribute $1,000,000s to the county. Please approve solar for Sedgwick County.”

So broadly speaking, everybody comes to the realization that building solar as well as wind is the right answer for their communities, and that’s because it’s by far the least expensive way to generate a kilowatt hour of electricity today. So those cost savings are going to trickle down to the consumer eventually. That’s difficult to back up with studies because the situations are so different, but example after example shows that communities fare well that have large-scale solar arrays in their energy infrastructure. Reasonable questions about any project should be carefully considered. Mr. Steve Howerton
, 8019 S. Meridian Ave., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’m going to be talking about primarily regulations on the BESS, but some other things may creep in here. The BESS are being introduced across the country at a rapid pace. However, in Kansas, and particularly Sedgwick County, the regulatory framework has not kept up with the technology. At the state level, the state level has not established comprehensive regulations specifically governing large-scale lithium battery storage. While the Kansas Corporation Commission oversees utility and grid connection, it has not adopted detailed statewide standards for safety siteing, emergency response, decommissioning, and long-term financial responsibility. At the local level, Sedgwick County currently has no standalone BESS regulations.”
Mr. Howerton
continued, “In general review, as part of larger solar projects or on a case-by-case basis, the state commission looks at the decommissioning, battery disposal, and financial assurances. The county itself recognizes these concerns by placing a one-year pause on future BESS facilities while officials evaluate potential impacts and determine what regulations are needed. That action acknowledges that important questions about safety, environmental protection, and long-term responsibility remain unsolved. These regulatory gaps create real risk. Lithium batteries can experience thermal runaways, resulting in fires that can burn at temperatures exceeding 2,000 degrees Fahrenheit, are difficult to extinguish, and can or may release toxic inflammable gasses. Without clear standards, local emergency responders may not have the training, equipment, or resources needed to respond effectively.”
Mr. Howerton
continued, “These sites of this size will contain 1,000,000 of these batteries that were spoke of earlier. That is 1,000,000 chances of a fire. There are also long-term financial and environmental concerns. Batteries eventually degrade and must be replaced, recycled, and disposed of. Without enforceable decommissioning requirements and adequate financial assurances, those costs could ultimately fall on local taxpayers and property owners. If Kansas has not yet adapted comprehensive regulations, and Sedgwick County has already concluded that additional study is necessary before approving future BESS facilities, then approving projects before these protections are in place creates unnecessary risk. If these reasons, technical testimony, regulatory deficiencies, and the real concerns raised by neighboring property owners, I respectfully urge the Commission to deny the approval of this project.”

Mr. Dusty Self
, 7150 S. 135th St. W., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I am opposed to the Galena and the TED Solar Energy Projects. I appreciate the opportunity to speak here today. I am not the only resident of the county, but also a real estate professional who has spent years working helping families buy, sell, and invest in property here. My perspective is based on working every day with homeowners, landowners, buyers, lenders, appraisers, and developers. I understand the importance of private property rights and economic development, but I also understand that the growth should strengthen a community rather than create long-term uncertainty. As a real estate agent, one of the most important lessons I’ve learned is that perception influences value. Buyers don’t purchase property based solely on square footage or acreage. They purchase neighborhoods, views, lifestyles, and confidence in the future of an area. When buyers hesitate, demand softens, and when demand softens, property values can be affected.”
Mr. Self
continued, “I have already seen this happen with my own eyes as I’ve been showing property near proposed data center sites and proposed solar energy fields, and that has been a question that these people have asked. Our county has built its identity over generations. We are known for productive farmland, open landscapes, and rural living, and communities where people choose to raise families or retire. Those qualities are valuable assets, and if we allow large sections of agricultural land to be converted into industrial energy facilities, that rural character changes, sometimes permanently for the duration of the leases and can last several decades. The zoning exists because not every land use belongs in every location. Industrial facilities are generally separated from residential neighborhoods because different uses have different impacts. Industrial-scale solar facilities occupy extensive acreage and involve substantial infrastructure, including the fencing, inverter stations, electrical equipment, maintenance access roads, and often new transmission connections. Regardless of whether they produce emissions, they function as an industrial land use rather than traditional agriculture, and that distinction matters.”
Mr. Self
continued, “Private property rights are important, but they exist alongside the responsibility of local government to protect the health, safety, and welfare of the broader community, including the equity that could be wiped out by this installation. Balancing those interests is one of your most important responsibilities. I can see this not having only an effect on the immediate surrounding properties, which I am located within 1,000 feet of one of the proposed sites, but I can also see this having a detrimental impact on Clearwater community and Haysville community as people look at those as potential for development and new housing usage. Thank you for your consideration.”

said, “Okay, any questions, anyone? All right, thank you very much. Councilmember, Haysville Councilmember Eric Myers, followed up by Kyle Kennedy. Good afternoon, Councilmember.”
Mr. Eric Myers
, City of Haysville Councilmember, greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’m the owner of Myers Mushrooms, LLC. I’m Haysville City Councilor Ward One, so northwest Haysville. I come to you speaking as an individual, not on behalf of the city. I am speaking in support of the Mission Project and in opposition to the TED Project as proposed. Full disclosure: I’m neighbors with the Bergkamp family, who are farmers applying for the Mission Project. Since I began farming in 2019, my company and theirs have worked together, helping one another as neighboring farms often do. My comments today, however, are based on a review of the project and the facts surrounding it, not that relationship. I went to the initial Mission proposal meeting, the opposition meeting, and I met with the farmers applying.”
Mr. Myers
continued, “I even spoke out against some aspects, such as the initially proposed Chinese components and foreign investments. Mission clarified that the panels will be USA-made in order to comply with federal tax credits. I also came to understand that who invests in a privately-funded project should have no impact on how they are governed unless sanctioned. In fact, allowing a strategic ally to invest wealth in Kansas enriches our country through infrastructure that should last for decades and help stabilize rising electrical costs. The Mission Project is positioned to utilize existing electrical transmission lines from the decommissioned gas power plant. Using existing infrastructure makes sense and benefits the local grid, shifting away from dependence on fossil fuels. The project’s battery storage is located within an established heavy industrial corridor. The presence of OxyChem and the elevator has shaped the character of this area for decades. Because of the nature of these hazardous industrial sites with a history of explosions, fire, and toxic releases, this is not an area that is expected or encouraged to become developed.”
Mr. Myers
continued, “I know as a property owner, a farmer, a business owner in Sedgwick County, I hope the Commissioners will allow these farmers to do what they choose with their land that they own, especially when you consider the benefits Sedgwick County as a whole. We currently have no utility-scale power generation in the county that I know of, and solar is by far the cheapest cost per kilowatt for production. For the TED Project, which I oppose, it encroaches on the Clearwater area of influence, and the Clearwater Council unanimously voted against this project through proclamation. I know if I was in their shoes as a Councilor, I would hope that that would be enough to send this project back to planning for the TED Project. Me addressing the Commissioners as a City Councilor, since coming onto the City Council last year, I have initiated a review of quotes and available federal credits, and I’m glad to announce that Haysville is going solar in 2027. The project will pay for itself in its energy offset in under 10 years with a 30-year lifespan. These systems work. The technology has advanced in durability, efficiency, and cost over the years, and it makes fiscal sense to invest in solar when possible. I would urge the Commissioners as well as surrounding municipalities to investigate going solar on any municipal infrastructure. This will absolutely aid financial stability to municipal energy budgets and help mitigate ever rising taxes. Thank you.”

That is a heavy industrial site, and that area should stay like that. We don’t ever want to develop homes there. We don’t even want to put businesses there other than the ones that are operating in those plants, because now you’re putting more people in a radius that could potentially get gassed out, burnt out. Those adopted positions guide our public support for responsible, sustainable energy policies. The League recently adopted an updated energy position that supports the development of utility-scale solar and wind energy with energy storage in Kansas. The position also supports continued growth of smaller distributed solar systems by individuals, businesses, schools, and government entities. The solar energy and the battery storage must be viewed as working partners. Together, they can bring a cleaner, more resilient electric grid for Sedgwick County.”
Ms. Thimesch
continued, “Our support for clean energy just isn’t a blanket approval for just any project. We strongly support timely public notice, accessible open hearings, transparency, and meaningful public participation on all energy decisions. Local standards should be based on sound science and developed with community input. They should address drainage and setbacks, and fire and emergency planning, the environmental impact, landscaping, and the safe removal or recycling of the equipment when it approaches the end of its useful life. Sedgwick County has an opportunity to support economic growth and responsible energy development at the same time. We encourage the Commission to create clear, fair, workable rules that allow well-planned solar and energy storage projects to move forward while protecting the nearby residents, landowners, and public safety. Please support responsible energy and battery storage as part of Sedgwick County’s long-term energy. Thank you so much for your time.”

Mr. Jordan Nolan
, 5410 E. Mainsgate Rd., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I am the president of this IBEW Local 271 right here in Wichita, Kansas. You see, our industry is incredibly diverse. We do everything from installing the ceiling fan and light switches in your bedroom to maintaining the 135 kV (kilovolt) to 69 kV substations on Boeing Wichita’s campus. We work on everything from computer jacks in the wall to the emergency life safety generators at Wesley Hospital. We are scaled, and we are diversified. I believe this solar project is no different. We cannot invest in only ceiling fans, we cannot invest in only emergency life safety generators, and we cannot invest in only substations. We need to diversify our energy portfolio if we are going to meet the demands of an ever-changing technological world, and build an energy infrastructure that is ready for what’s to come next.”

continued, “There’s another reason I believe this project is worth considering. It’s an investment in the people who live here. The paycheck goes home to a family. It pays a mortgage. It buys groceries. It takes a little boy out to see the Wind Surge. It buys a little girl a bag of fish food at the Sedgwick County Zoo. It gets spent at our local businesses that make this community what it is, and when we create those opportunities here, we keep that investment right here. We don’t just live here. We work here, we raise our families here, we spend our money here, and we invest in this community every single day.”

Mr. Mike Foulk
, 13806 W. MacArthur Rd., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’ve installed and maintained 2 solar power production systems for the last 15 years, 1 with energy storage. These are used for apprenticeship training at the Wichita Electrical Training Center, where I’m an instructor. It’s estimated that enough solar energy hits our planet in one hour to power the entire globe for a full year. Think about that for a second. We do not have an energy shortage in the world. We lack the ability to use what we already have available to us. As of 2025, the U.S. Energy Information Administration reports approximately 7 percent of our electricity came from solar. This number is double the amount that’s generated from solar in 2023. This shows more growth in energy production than any other sector.”
Mr. Foulk
continued, “I have heard my neighbors’ many concerns about the project, and I would like to address a few. One is the concern that usable farmland is being taken out of production. If this is really the concern, then why aren’t we currently protesting the constant development and urban sprawl? I have lived at my current residence for the last 20 years. In that time frame, I have seen countless developments that have gobbled up acres of active farmland. There are currently three developments of high-end homes within two miles of me, and another two developments with duplexes. Once developed, this land will never revert back to agricultural use. However, a solar farm near the end of its operational life could be returned back to farmland, or the solar panels could be upgraded to continue supplying clean energy to our grid. I think a large solar plant like this could be seen as a land bank that actually preserves farmland for future use.”
Mr. Foulk
continued, “Another concern is the electricity produced will not be used here. This is simply not how the electric grid works. That power will be put on the grid and used. Putting another 260 megawatts of electricity on our grid will make our electrical supply more robust and more reliable because our energy portfolio will be diversified. Solar also has the benefit of peak production during the hours and times a year when we use the most electricity. Another concern is the solar plant will be unsightly to look at. There are a limited number of homes near the site, and I empathize with these people’s concerns. I would ask that the green buffer zones be strictly enforced around these homes where their line of sight is being impacted. In short, I believe the economic benefit, creation of jobs, increased reliability of our grid, and preservation of agricultural land for future use justifies this project. I support it and encourage you to support it as well.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Thank you, Mike. Any questions? All right, next speaker, Vicki Winchell, followed by Carmaine Ternes. Good afternoon, Vicki.”

Mr. JC Moore
, 146 S. Maize Rd. #5, greeted the Commissioners and said, “I know that the people living in the Clearwater area are quite concerned about the technology and the safety of the solar panels because they’re fairly new. I’m a chemist, and I wanted to just tell them a little bit that I know about the panels. I looked at the composition of the panels, and I looked at the composition of the batteries, and the leach tests. Leach tests are where they immerse them in water and then see what leaks out. All of those were negative. The panels will not leach out anything that will damage the soil or that will damage or affect the groundwater. So, and the same way is for the batteries, that’s also true. The batteries are the new lithium iron phosphate batteries, and those are much less likely to overheat than the older kind of lithium batteries, which were a different style. The way they’re deployed, they put, instead of 1 big 250 volt megawatt batteries, they are putting 250 small ones. In that arrangement, if one of the batteries would happen to overheat, it would not catch any of the other batteries on fire. It’s very unlikely that one would overheat.”
Mr. Moore
continued, “I talked to an expert in battery fire safety. He was a fireman, and he said that if there was a fire in the battery, they didn’t need to use perfluoro alkanes (perfluoroalkoxy alkanes) to put it out. All they needed to use was water, and they didn’t spray it on the battery. They just needed to wet the area around it down so that the grass fires wouldn’t spread, and wait for the battery to burn itself up. So, and the materials when the battery burns is probably a little bit from the plastic coating, which would be like burning a piece of plastic or something like that. I wanted to talk a little about our energy needs for the future. They’re growing rapidly. We’re predicting that we’ll have great energy needs that are coming up, and I wonder how do you think we should meet those?”
Mr. Moore
continued, “Well, we can build more fossil fuel plants. Evergy had a good plan. They’re building two new gas-fired power plants to power AI (Artificial Intelligence) centers. Guess who’s paying for them? If you’re an Evergy customer, you had 8.5 percent added to your bill this last month to pay for those data centers, even though they don’t have a customer yet for the data centers. The fossil fuel companies, or like the coal companies, are very polluting. They put a lot of pollution in the air, and as far as cost goes, the levelized cost of energy for coal is about 13 cents a kilowatt hour. For natural gas, it’s about eight, and for solar, it’s about six cents, so solar is much less expensive. Besides that, we don’t have to pay for the plants. The developers or the investors are going to pay for those. So we will. Oh, am I supposed to stop? Are you?”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Yeah, whenever you’re ready.”
Mr. Moore
said, “Okay, can I get my closing line here?”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Yeah, give it to us.”
Mr. Moore
said, “So the MAPC drew up guidelines for the solar companies, and the County Commissioners approved it, and their plan meets every one of those guidelines. I think it’s up to you to approve it. So thank you.”

said, “Okay, any questions for JC?”

Check your air index quality in Wichita and notice what it is today versus 10 or 20 years ago. It’s only going up. So let’s talk about the future costs and the cost of energy. Solar and battery storage are only getting cheaper every single year. I have a report and a cited work for that if you like, Commissioner. $128.27, that was the cost of 1 watt, to produce 1 watt of solar in 1975. Today, it’s 26 cents. So the cost of solar is undeniably going down. Unlike gas, diesel, oil, and coal, the cost continues to be more reliable, and the longevity of panels continue to get better and better.”

Expanding to a 1 mile radius, that number increases roughly to 103 family homes.”

continued, “Safety and proximity to schools: there is documented fire risk for solar farms, as reported by Fire Trace International. A fire at this facility, combined with prevailing south winds, would direct a hazardous smoke plume straight into the school and surrounding neighborhood of Wheatland. Furthermore, Campus High School, Haysville Middle School, St. Cecilia Catholic School in Haysville, Alternate High School Haysville, and St. Peter’s Catholic School are all located within two to three miles of the BESS. Potential industrial accidents: during 1998, with the DeBruce Grain Elevator explosion, we experienced an industrial catastrophe directly across the street from where I live. The BESS location is less than 0.5 mile from our residence. The potential exists for another industrial catastrophic event due to minimal regulatory oversight parameters. Lack of regulatory oversight: the Mission Clean Energy application is reportedly grandfathered in for BESS. It is deeply concerning for the Commission to consider an approval vote. Mr. Russell Fox
, 605 N. Westlink Ave., greeted the Commissioners and said, “One of the things that were listed on the form that we had to fill out to become a speaker here was try not to repeat stuff that has already been said. Try to add something new. We’ve had an enormous amount of feedback, and there’s still more feedback to come. So I tried to think about what I could say that might be a little bit different. I think all of you are aware that I run the history and politics major at Friends University. So I can put on my political science hat, and I can speak really briefly to something that’s fairly common amongst those of us nerds that really, you know, get into politics, and government, and election. That is that representatives like yourselves sometimes act like delegates. That is, you understand yourselves as people who are voting directly in the stead of people that can’t come to governing meetings. They can’t go to Topeka. They can’t go to Washington D.C. The goal is to mirror directly what the constituents ask for.”
Dr. Fox
continued, “That’s the delegate model, but there is also the trustee model. The trustee model is the assumption that it’s not just that you’re in a position to do something that many other citizens can’t do. It’s also that you have access to specialists, to expertise. You have the ability to put together meetings like these and collect a large amount of feedback, and then you make a judgment call. You’re not making a decision directly in the stead of someone else, but you’re making a decision on behalf of someone else, and the communities, and the culture that they represent. Obviously, neither those models necessarily tell you exactly how you would vote on a complicated issue like this. I really appreciate the fact that over the course of the morning into the afternoon we’ve, you know, talked about the Golden Rule of Zoning. We’ve talked about land and water use. We’ve talked about safety. We’ve talked about access. We’ve talked about pollination and agriculture, energy, property values, and more.”
Dr. Fox
continued, “I do not envy the difficult decision that all of you have to make. I would simply, as a Sedgwick County resident and as someone who is deeply concerned about some broader trends in energy use and in environmental concerns, for you to think about the larger issues. Everybody knows that there is a transformation taking place in how we develop and how we use energy. Commissioner Baty, you put EV (Electric Vehicle) chargers on your mattress store. When my daughter Kristen, who had to leave for work, went to buy a car, the first car they tried to sell her was an EV. This transformation is ongoing.”
Dr. Fox
continued, “Let me just finish really quickly. Industry knows it. Unions know it. Policymakers know it. Haysville knows it. That does not necessarily mean that you have to disregard all of the particular concerns that might be expressed by people that could be directly impacted by any particular development. Of course, you have to address those, and there are policies in place for you to do so, and there are ways in those policies and those regulations can be adapted and updated as time goes by. Please don’t forget the trustee model. Please don’t forget your responsibility to not just reflect specific, addressable concerns, but also take a look at the big picture. Putting in solar power installations is a step towards addressing a necessary future that all of us are already in the midst of going through, and I hope that that will guide your decision today.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Thank you, Dr. Fox. Any questions?”

Ms. Jennifer Connelly
, 3353 N. 159th St. E., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’m glad to be here for all of you. I just want to start out by: we used to run an Airbnb. I wasn’t going to bring this up, but we had two fire watchers from the refinery in El Dorado stay with us. They were young girls, and the refinery has fire watchers all the time because refineries catch on fire. So that’s a, but you know me, I like to tell jokes, and you’ve heard part of this one before. Now I have a bad memory, so I’m going to read my own joke. Now you’ve all heard about Ray the solar panel who walks into the bar and asks the bartender, why can’t I get a date? The bartender says, I don’t know, Ray. You always seem to have a dark cloud hanging over your head. I have great news. Ray met the love of his life. Her name is Bess. That’s short for battery energy storage. The second they met, electrons started flowing. I heard tell that Ray has asked Bess to marry him. He said, Bess, won’t you marry me? I feel like your electrons and my electrons are a perfect match. The dark clouds don’t seem to matter. When I have you, I just feel like we’ll connect so well. I’ve heard tell there’s 1,000s of Rays and Besses across the country. Now that’s a beautiful thing.”
Ms. Connelly
continued, “I just want to note some facts here. According to the U.S. Energy Information Administration, since 2026 just now, 7.8 gigawatt hours of energy has been produced for this year, and they are expecting 86 gigawatt hours of energy to be produced for the rest of the year. 28 percent of that is expected to come from battery storage, 51 percent from solar, and only less than 7 percent from natural gas. Mr. Kent Rowe
, 3353 N. 159th St. E., greeted the Commissioners and said, “I appear here today on behalf of National Emergency Management Awareness Month. Because, if you’ll refer to this picture on the screens in the right lower corner, you will see a facility there. That is formerly Vulcan Materials, that is Oxy Chemicals. The proposed battery installation is well-suited for this particular location, owing to OxyChem’s bulk storage, manufacturing, and transportation of chlorinated halogenated compounds inside an evacuation alert radius. Setback distances from such facilities are presently being sustained. The Chlorine Institute, Chemical Safety Board, EPA Risk Management Rule 40 CFR (Code of Federal Regulations) Part 68, OSHA (Occupational Safety and Health Administration) Process Safety Management 20 CFR 1910.119, NFPA (National Fire Protection Association) 400 mandate distance to endpoint for a worst case toxic release, which can span several miles.”
Mr. Rowe
continued, “An emergency alert system must continue to be maintained surrounding this facility, 1 of 30 such facilities across the United States. Zoning applications for schools, hospitals, daycare centers must not be approved within this radius. Even Light Commercial operations within an urban area of growth must not be located within this region because off-site worker populations lack personal protective equipment, training, and evacuation procedures following uncontrolled releases of chlorine, hydrochloric acid, and other chloralkali products. By the way, this facility manufactures 237 metric tons per year. Chlorine mishaps have increased 61 percent from 2015 to 2022. A sober reminder of two mishaps here in Kansas was the toxic release of chlorine, forcing the shelter-in-place and evacuation of 11,000 residents in Atchison, Kansas in 2016. The DeBruce grain dust explosion in 1998 killed seven workers and saw slabs of concrete landing inside the boundaries of this chemical facility.”
Mr. Rowe
continued, “Therefore, an urban area of growth cannot statutorily be designated to this region. Agricultural landowners would be therefore deprived of selling their land for urban development. They have a unique right to dual use of their land outside the purview of an urban area of growth. Please approve the solar and battery energy storage system for this region to create a setback region around this plant that has a very excellent safety record, but they will tell you things happen. Personally, I worked in the chemical and petrochemical industry. Things do happen, usually human factors, human cause. Thank you all.”

Mr. Waggoner
said, “Yeah, I wanted to just touch on the fact. As you all know, you’re sitting as a quasi-judicial body hearing this zoning case. One, the first thing I wanted to add a comment on is the applicant’s agent had contemplated adding a condition to any potential approval you might have on this that the applicant would not seek any tax benefits that they might be able to qualify for under state law. There would be some concerns. I would suggest against adding that as a condition. If they would, let’s say, for example, if this was to be approved, and they wish to do that, I think they would have their own discretion to not seek those tax benefits. I would not suggest adding it as a condition of approval.”
Mr. Waggoner
continued, “Again, I think that’s something they could pursue on their own. If you have, I also would point out that if that condition was added, there’s a pretty good chance it would not be enforceable. Some of those benefits, and you heard Brent describe the complexity that’s involved, but some of those don’t even require an application. They just happen as a matter under state law, matter of course, and so there’s no enforcement ability on the county’s part because it would just happen automatically under state law, especially maybe some of the BESS CIME exemption. So I just mention that to say that I don’t know that it would be enforceable. If you have further questions on that in particular, let me know. I could elaborate further. I just don’t think it’s a condition that could be part of this. Again, if they wanted to do that on their own volition, I think that’s something they could do. I would not suggest it be a condition of approval if you ever move to that route.”
Mr. Waggoner
continued, “A few other things, Chairman and Commissioners: there’s a few things about the company that have been mentioned in a lot of the public comments. It’s fine that the public’s making those comments, but I did at least want to mention in your consideration of the case a few things about the company or aspects of their project that I think probably don’t have much relevance in terms of a zoning standpoint and where you’re sitting. First, there was a comment that this was the first of its type that this company’s done before. Don’t know that that’s particularly relevant. Generally speaking, when you’re considering a zoning case, you’re evaluating the reasonableness of the use for the particular property involved and the surrounding property. You’re really judging the use, not the applicant.”
Mr. Waggoner
continued, “Also, there’s been some comment that the company may be foreign-owned. True or not, or foreign-owned, domestic-owned, that’s not really something that I would suggest as having a relevant consideration in your consideration of this case. I think similar to that, there were questions and comments about if the panels were made in other countries. I mean those are things you don’t usually get into on zoning cases, and I would worry that if that is something that you would consider, that would not be a sound basis for an approval or denial.”
Mr. Waggoner
continued, “Also, the question about whether, there had been some comments both here and at the Planning Commission about if the power would go elsewhere. If it would go out of state is probably the biggest concern. There’s the Interstate Commerce Clause in the Constitution. If that’s a factor in your decision, there could be a concern that your decision could get overturned because violating that Commerce Clause. So I would suggest that you would not take that into account in your decision, again, because I think that could give a legal grounds for a challenge.”
Mr. Waggoner
continued, “Last point I had, Commissioners, was, you know, there was also some discussion about maybe adding a condition to make the applicant have to hire local workforce. I don’t think that’s a condition you should include. That’s probably not a reasonable condition. I think that’s just kind of outside of the zoning context on this. I think you can consider the economic effect of the project in any direction, including, you know, jobs it may create or, you know, there can be pros and cons to that. I think you can evaluate all of that, but I would not suggest adding a condition that would require an applicant to hire a certain workforce for a lot of reasons. I’d be happy to stand for any questions on those things, Commissioner. Thank you.”

VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Wadle
continued, “In this area, it recommends essentially Rural Residential, which is agricultural or residential. In our county, we don’t have a distinction. We don’t have a zoning district just for agricultural. So when we start looking at in the future what will happen, we leave it pretty general in terms of agricultural or residential. The other factor to think about is that is it in an urban growth area, number one. This has been a discussion. A part of the big discussion here is is this going to be some other future land use? Our Comprehensive Plan does not show this in an urban growth area for a city. So then you’re left with the distinction of, okay, is it an allowable use? Is this a good use to have it as a utility, kind of civic public use in the form of this scale of solar project? That’s where in the zoning regulations there are mitigating factors and conditions that are applied. The question is just whether or not those are sufficient to make it a good use of the land.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “Yeah, okay. That’s helpful. Okay, I’m going to make a quick statement, and then I’m going to listen to my colleagues’ questions before I formulate, and I’m willing to make motions whenever, after everybody listens. Every zoning case is a balance between things: community impact, and property rights. I mean, every single zoning case it feels. Some of these are really easy decisions. Some of these are really hard decisions like today. This is a difficult one. I think some of the things that we’re trying to balance is, you know, I think the easy thing to do would say, well, we have this many people showed up, and they’re against it, and this many people showed up, and they’re for it, and the ‘ayes’ take it or the ‘nos’ take it. The only problem with that is that’s void of principle. There’s no principle there.”
Commissioner Baty
continued, “That would be easy, but that’s not the way things work, because the truth of this matter is that when there’s no direction, then we have to fall back onto principles, and we have to ask really hard questions. Some of the questions that I’ve been asking and jotting notes the whole time is what really is the highest and best use of this land? That’s a very important question. I’ve heard several speakers mention it. Then the question is who gets to make that decision? Who gets to make the decision on what is the highest and the best use of the land? Is it the county? Is that our job to make the use of what the highest and best use of your land is? Is it the landowners, or is it their neighbors? Who gets to make the determination of what the highest and best use of the land is? That’s a question I’ve been laboring over here. What really is the environmental and the community impact? We’ve talked about property values. We’ve talked about environment. We’ve talked about water. What really is the community impact, a question I’ve been laboring over. The other question is is there a way that we can protect everyone’s interest here? Is there really a way we can protect everyone’s interest?”
Commissioner Baty
continued, “Lastly is we built a policy. We had a year of building a policy. How much weight do we put into the policy that we built, and there were applications that spent $1,000,000s to try to come here, and our policy turned them away a year and a half ago. So we’ve put a lot of weight and a lot of time into building policy. How much weight do we put into the policy that we built, and does this application fit the policy that we built? These are things that I’m thinking about. In the end, the challenge is, you know, can I look these people in their eye and say, what you’re saying to me doesn’t matter. I can’t do that. I can’t do that. It does, and you alluded to it too. I can’t look these people in the eye and say that their concerns don’t have merit. I can’t, but I can’t look these farmers in the eye either and tell them that their property rights don’t matter, and that their economic options should only be limited to farming.”
Commissioner Baty
continued, “I won’t do that because I know how hard it is in farming right now. I know the challenge. Their rising costs, they have drought. They have water concerns. They have succession planning concerns. They have, our farmers are hurting. They’re hurting all over the country, but they’re hurting in Kansas. They’re hurting in Sedgwick County, and these families are concerned. We had an individual that didn’t show up today for public comment that emailed us and said that when she had an opportunity to join your program, she thought it was a gift from God because financially their family wasn’t making it. So I look everyone in the eye and have honest conversations about how difficult this is. Right here on this collaboration, we have 14 property owners. Is that the number?”
Mr. Frazier
said, “Yes. There might be a little bit of changes if you’re divvying it up from like who owns LLCs, but yeah. Generally speaking, yes.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “So we have about 14 property owners, give or take, making up this application. The purpose of a Conditional Use permit is to try to find a workable solution that protects the property rights of these applicants with their neighbors so that they can coexist. That’s what we’re trying to accomplish today, and that’s not easy. It’s certainly not going to be universally accepted. I’ve come to understand that, and I understand everyone’s angst and everyone’s concerns. All of it is merited, and all of it should be considered. In the end, for me, I’ve got to find a way in a Conditional Use to mitigate concerns and protect the overarching prevailing principle in all of this, and that principle for me is property rights. That principle for me is protecting landowner property rights, so that they can make economic decisions on their land. So this is what I’m trying to do now, is to mitigate all the community concerns to find that balance, because the prevailing principle in all of this: we can’t separate liberty and property rights. They’re inseparable. It’s a prevailing principle that’s going to guide my discussion and my thought process and the way I vote today.”
Commissioner Baty
continued, “So, if we strip that away, property rights from landowners to make economic decisions on their property, if we strip those things away, we are basically letting them be at the mercy of an omnipotent government, and I’m just not comfortable with that. I am simply going to defend landowners and property rights every chance I get and try to mitigate the concerns of the community around it to find a balance. So, I’m going to be supportive of this project. I’m going to be supportive of landowner rights, but I’m only going to do so. Be very clear. I’m only going to do so if we get some reinforced conditions and mitigations. If we’re able to accomplish that here, then I’ll be supportive of this application and supportive of the landowners and their property rights. I’m going to stand. I’m maybe not done with questions yet. I’m going to listen to my colleagues for questions, but that’s kind of where my head’s at as we stand right now.”

said, “So I think there’s two pieces to that. The first one is that at least in the responding fire department, Sedgwick Rural Fire District One is one of the tax receiving entities that, you know, gets a significant amount of revenue over the life. You know, it’s not, you know, $100,000,000, right, of course, but it is a significant number. I think as it relates to, you know, requirements for services provided, I think, you know, similar to a road use agreement, it would make sense as some sort of signed agreement to cover those costs that are incurred, particularly as it relates to construction when there are more staff on site. Security provisions are probably less strict, and I see Scott standing up, so I believe he has something to say, but that would be our recommendation at least, assuming it’s not explicitly noted in the staff report, which I will cede to Scott now.”

If there’s money coming to the county or the Fire District directly, I’d have concerns about that. So I would not suggest that. Now, what I think applicants’ agent is talking about, and similar to the road use agreement that’s contemplated within your conditions of approval, you could have, if you have very specific things that the county, like for example, materials for roads. You know, the road use agreement contemplates that they would have to provide that kind of stuff. So the county wouldn’t be out the money for that.”
Mr. Waggoner

MOTION
Chairman Blubaugh moved to override the recommendation of the MAPC and deny the zone change for the following reasons based on the Golden factors, with subsequent findings to support the motion. The character of the neighborhood is not consistent with proposed uses. The zoning uses and nearby properties are not consistent with the proposed use. Lastly, there is significant neighborhood opposition to this application. The following findings are made as part of this motion. There is significant neighborhood opposition, as reflected by the protest petitions that amount to approximately 42.6 percent of the notice area of this case, testimony at the MAPC meeting, a significant majority of which indicated opposition to the application and the number of comments against the application received today. The character of the neighborhood is largely agricultural with single-family housing. The application would introduce a large-scale use for solar and a battery energy system to an industrial use that would be approximately 1,500 acres in size and would not be consistent with the existing character of that neighborhood.
A majority of the neighboring properties that are zoned Rural Residential and SF-20 Single-Family are utilized for agricultural purposes and single-family housing. The proposed Conditional Use contemplates a use that is significantly more intensive than those in most nearby properties, making the zoning and uses out of the steps of those already found in the portion of unincorporated Sedgwick County. The bulk of the application is currently zoned for Rural Residential and Single-Family 20 houses. Denial of the application would enable the properties to continue to be utilized for agriculture and residential purposes, which would continue to be viable for the property. Nearby properties run the risk of having their property valuations negatively impacted if the applications would be approved. A study completed by the Virginia Tech in 2025 indicated that the residential houses located within 3 miles of a solar site would experience a 4.8 percent reduction in value. There are several homes located along the periphery of this application area and well within three miles, so this application would present some concerns and reduce valuations.
The application area is just outside of the City of Wichita Urban Growth Area. Within the Comprehensive Plan, the growth area is identified as the growth area through 2035 or the next nine years. The solar application has been proposed for to last approximately 40 years. If approved, the solar application would act as an impediment to growth for this portion of unincorporated area for more than 30 years beyond the time frame contemplated within the urban growth area, meaning that would be an area that the City of Wichita might otherwise reasonably expect to grow within that window of time. It is found that aesthetics of the rows upon rows of solar panels, which will all likely have a certain level of visibility even with the screening that is contemplated, will not align with the rural characteristics of the application area.
Commissioner Wise seconded the motion.

The MAPC closed the public hearing and voted to defer the application to a later date, their June 25th meeting, with a request that Planning Department staff provide additional follow-up info. On May 26th, the City of Clearwater approved a resolution expressing opposition to this application. That resolution is attached as one of the attachments to the staff report. On June 25th, the MAPC considered this application for the second time, and the follow-up information. No additional public comment was received because the public hearing was closed. The MAPC voted to approve the application, subject to staff recommended conditions, and that vote was six, four, one. There was one abstention, and that was because that Planning Commissioner was not present at the first meeting when they considered this application, and so they opted to sit this one out. The number of recommended conditions for this one is 39, and they include a variety of conditions, including landscaping, environmental health, decommissioning, health and safety, and more.”
Mr. Wadle
continued, “In terms of protests, the number of protests received against this application was 103. That equaled 71 percent of the net protest area, which exceeds the state threshold of 20 percent. So it requires a supermajority or four of five votes in order to approve this Conditional Use. So the recommended action coming to you from the MAPC is to adopt the findings of the MAPC, approve the Conditional Use with the recommended conditions, authorize the Chairman to sign the resolution, and authorize the resolution to be published. Again, requires four out of five votes. Alternatives include the following: you can override the MAPC, adopt alternative findings, and deny the application. That would require a two-thirds majority or four out of five votes because it’s an override. As always, you can return the case to the MAPC for further consideration with a statement specifying the basis for the failure to approve or deny the application, and that would just require a simple majority or three of five votes. Mr. Shelton
said, “This one’s a little simpler in terms of the fiscal impacts to local governments than the previous one because it is simply a solar project. So it’s a little more straightforward. The project investment is about $110,000,000 in solar equipment. That would be eligible then for the solar exemption for 10 years. That $110,000,000 investment gets depreciated down to 20 percent of that, then times the assessment rate of 25 percent. So you basically have $5,500,000 of assessed value going on the tax roll in year 11. As we discussed before, the underlying real estate would be reclassified from agricultural use to a commercial use class. That will typically cause that value of that land to increase.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “As I said earlier, we have an opinion, not a directive, or not a formal guidance from Property Valuation Division, that the land is also exempt during that 10-year period of time. So in year 11, it moves forward. Everything comes back on the tax roll for this project and the projected. Let me back up one thing. During that 10-year period, they have pledged $385,000 a year for payment in lieu of tax during that period of time. So that’s $3,800,000 that this would generate during the first 10 years. Then, doing the math with the typical mill levy, we’re looking at about $660,000 a year in property tax generation from this solar facility. Questions on that?”

said, “Brent, did you do a sheet on this like you did the other one?”

I think we’ve used about 120 mills for that calculation. If a similar commercial investment were to be made, and let’s put it subject to a 10-year tax exemption, it would generate $220,000 per year for the first 10 years. That would all go to the school capital outlay fund, nothing to the county, or the Fire District, or anyone else. When it’s fully on the tax roll, you’re looking at about $2,900,000 a year in tax generation. So it would be a more lucrative to the public sector type of investment than a solar farm.”
Mr. Shelton
continued, “Residential, what we did was took, let’s say, over the period of over 20 years, if it built out enough residential to equal that $110,000,000 investment, because probably not going to get that many houses that fast. So we just prorated it over 20 years, and you can see those results in the top graph, that in year one it’s generating about $66,000 in total property tax. It goes all the way down to about $1,300,000 per year in year 20. So, as I mentioned before, the agriculture land, generating about $5,500 a year in tax. If it goes to solar, you’ve got the projections there: $385,000 for 10 years and then $661,000, and then a potential commercial investment with tax abatement for 10 years or just straight residential development kind of coming online over the next 20 years. So those were the assumptions that went into that chart. It’s broken down further on there for specific to county, county fire, school, and so forth.”
