Mr. Ben Metcalf,
Vice President – Project Development of TED Renewables, greeted the Commissioners and said, “I’m here today on behalf of Branch Line Solar and the six landowning families who are our co-applicants and partners. They have leased us the 1,360 acres that are in our application. That gives us the project design flexibility for the 650 acres that this project will ultimately occupy once it’s built. We are here today because of them. Now I want to be clear on the outset about what this vote is about and what it’s not about. This vote is not about solar versus some other hypothetical land use that would require these families to sell their land. These families have chosen not to sell their land but to lease their land and partner with Branch Line Solar. So this vote is on a narrower and more important question: whether six farm families who own land here, who pay taxes on it, and followed every rule this county set, have the right to decide how to use it. That’s the question.”
VISUAL PRESENTATION
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “So let me tell you who they are, because this argument doesn’t belong to me. It belongs to them. They will live with your decision long after I’ve gone home. I work for them today. Bob Hay has farmed here his whole life, was trustee of Ohio Township for 16 years, and always been a big supporter of this community. He was the first to partner with us back in 2023. Bob wanted to be here today, but he fell ill this morning, and let us know that he could not make it. Marion Barrow is a retired teacher and farmer, having taught in Haysville schools for 15 years and farmed in Sedgwick County with his late wife for over 40 years. He owns 40 acres involved with the project that is currently farmed by another one of our partners. This land has been in his family for decades.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “David and Debbie Klausmeyer grew up farming and farm today. They also operate a beloved pumpkin patch. David and Debbie’s two sons farm with them. Adam teaches school here. He and his wife Jessica partnered on 78 acres. Scott and his wife Carrie partnered on 116 acres. Both families saw the same thing: diversification and long-term stability for their children. The Struthers family has farmed the same ground since 1893, through the Depression, through the farm prices of the ‘80s, through every commodity cycle since. Wes Struthers farms it today. He isn’t here asking you for a favor. He’s simply asking to be able to make one more decision on land that his family has stewarded for over a century. Six farm families, deep roots in this county, every one of them made a decision about their land under rules this county wrote. Some of them will be speaking and telling their story today.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “As a kid, I loved going to my cousin’s house when I grew up. My uncle raised hogs and crops like his father and grandfather before him. I have never met a harder worker. I was in high school when they sold it. Hog prices had stayed down for too many years in a row. There’s only so long a family can scrape by. So when a farm family finds a way to put a floor under their operation, on their own ground, inside the rules, I don’t believe government should be the one that takes that away. So now let me tell you the story of how Branch Line came to be. In the summer of 2023, we found available capacity on the 138 kV line that runs through Ninnescah Township to the substation north of the Occidental Chemical Plant. More importantly, Bob Hay wanted to partner with us and host the project. We filed our interconnection application with Southwest Power Pool that fall.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “That same season, this Board placed a six-month moratorium on solar. We kept investing, and we waited. Your Planning Department did the work: research, an open house, a public survey, months of consensus buildings with cities and advisory boards on setbacks, size, configuration, and urban growth. The MAPC approved that 13 to 1. This Board went further. They extended the moratorium for another six months, hired a consulting firm. In September 2024, adopted a still more restrictive ordinance four to one. Under the new ordinance, solar was no longer allowed in urban growth areas. As you can see, about two-thirds of our project, the land that Bob Hay had signed with us, was within an urban growth area.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “By then, we were 18 months into the second of three phases of SPP study, with $1,000,000s in financial security posted and at risk, but the real cost wasn’t ours. Bob Hay had lost the right to use his ground. Unless we could find new landowners along the same transmission line whose land complied with the new ordinance, Branch Line Solar would be regulated out of business. Bob Hay already was. We didn’t come back asking for a waiver or an exemption. We rebuilt the project to fit your rules. Five new families came forward, and Bob Hay had one more parcel adjacent to the new project area. Branch Line Solar is the project it is today because it’s what this new ordinance created.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “Now you heard a different application this morning. I won’t characterize it or your decision on it. I’d only ask that Branch Line and the decision of these six families be decided on their own. Branch Line started development before this ordinance was written. When the new ordinance made our original project area out of compliance, we didn’t ask for a waiver. We moved to follow the ordinance. We have gone through three years of engineering studies with Evergy and Southwest Power Pool. Our interconnection agreement is ready to be signed. Our project is ready to be built, and to begin delivering power to support our economy and our way of life. This is not a speculative project. Every lights that switches on, every factory that produces goods, a grain elevator that processes food, they all rely on electricity. If we choose not to build new power plants, we are choosing to have less power, which means more expensive power, which means limited economic growth. You cannot have it both ways.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “Now, briefly on Clearwater, I want to be respectful. The city has every right to be heard, and the Council is doing what it believes is right for its residents, but I’d ask you to hold two things at once. The people who own this ground are your constituents too. Some of these families have been here for generations, and this decision rests with you, not the city. Clearwater isn’t buying the ground. It isn’t annexing it, it doesn’t tax it, and still the city has adopted a resolution asking you to take the extraordinary act of overturning a decision these families made about land the city doesn’t own because someday it might want it. Now I am not making a legal argument here, but to these families, this feels a lot like eminent domain just without the check. I’d be doing them a disservice if I didn’t say that today. This county did the hard work, spent over a year writing an ordinance to service residents. Your staff spent months reviewing our application, found it met your standards, and recommended it to the MAPC with conditions. We’ve accepted every condition your staff recommended. The MAPC held a five-hour public hearing, deliberated, deferred, met again, and ultimately agreed with your staff. They recommended to approve Branch Line Solar. They affirmed the property rights of these six families.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “Some final thoughts: we have spent $2,000,000 on this project since your ordinance was passed. We spent it because your ordinance said we could, because we believed it meant something. $550,000 of that is a non-refundable deposit on a transformer. $3,500,000 more is posted with SPP and is at risk today. Branch Line is a $175,000,000 project ready to build, but our interconnection agreement has to be signed by Friday. A delay isn’t a delay. It’s a denial. We’re not asking you to rush. We’re asking you for a decision either way. If you approve, we sign on Friday and pay Evergy the first installment of $17,000,000 to connect our project to the grid. If this Board overturns an application that met every requirement in your code, then the code isn’t a standard. It’s a suggestion. That should worry every landowner in this county, not just these six. Either the code means something, or it doesn’t. Property rights didn’t start with this ordinance. They’re why the Struthers family broke this ground in 1893, and why they’re still farming it today. I urge you, uphold this ordinance that these families followed. Stand with them and leave no doubt that in Sedgwick County, a family’s land is still their own.”

Within his presentation or his 10 minutes, it was implied that because we have regulations in place for a Conditional Use permit for this land use, that somehow we don’t have a authority to vote on or hear these cases. Can you just kind of walk through what legalities there are between having regulations in place for consideration versus what’s an automatic approval?”
Mr. Waggoner
said, “Certainly, Commissioner Wise. Understand the argument the applicant’s making on this, but I also would point out just because the Board has adopted regulations on this, there’s still a requirement. It doesn’t allow this to just be approved by right. It’s not like, they have to go through a process. There’s a zoning and notice process. It is a Conditional Use application where a decision has to be made. You’re sitting in a quasi-judicial capacity. So it doesn’t mean that just any up to 1,500 acre area in the unincorporated area is just approved if it meets the requirements. It also has to be, under the Golden factors you analyze the use, whether it’s a reasonable use for that location, whether the Golden factors weigh in favor of being a reasonable use, or whether they don’t. You can consider other factors in addition to the Golden factors. The Kansas appellate court case law says that. So it’s still very much an open case for you to consider just like every zoning case you guys consider. If it was by right, you wouldn’t be here hearing the case today. So does that answer your question, Commissioner Wise?”
Commissioner Wise
said, “Yes, it did.”
Mr. Waggoner

Specifically, I’m going to discuss criteria A and G, also known as Golden Rules Number One and Seven. Golden Rule One asks a simple question: does the proposed use fit the existing zoning, the uses of the ground, and the existing character of the area? The answer here is no.”

Mr. Woods

Little history on the Golden Rules of Zoning, just for the record, the Golden Rules were developed as a result of a 1978 Supreme Court case, Golden versus Overland Park, whereby the justices found in favor of the plaintiff and developed a specific set of guidelines that must be met in any zoning change. Now I’ll be covering Number Three of the zoning code, which reads: extent to which removal of the restrictions will detrimentally affect nearby property. Now you may find this interesting, but I am in favor of individual property rights as long as it doesn’t infringe upon the rights of others. If enacted, based on the studies that we research, the property owners within a mile will lose between 6 to 30 percent of their property value, with statistically significant reductions going out to 3 miles. Now I can’t think of too many worse detrimental impacts than the loss of home values, since the majority of people carry a significant portion of their total net worth in their home values. Based on the failure to meet the Unified Zoning Code or the Golden Rules, I request respectfully that the Commissioners vote for denial. Thank you.”

Imagine losing, I don’t know, say your house is worth $750,000 by the time you’re ready to sell it. Imagine losing $250,000, right, of your investment, your retirement. I mean this is very impactful to us that are very close to it. So I would ask you to please consider denying this application.”

Mr. Wadle

Commissioner Baty

Ms. Roubideaux
said, “I’m going to try to take out a few things since we’ve covered so many, and I don’t want to duplicate a whole bunch of it. Some unfortunately may come out that way. I am writing this to express our strong opposition to the proposed TED Renewables Branch Line Industrial Solar Project and any industrial solar, wind, or data that can come to Sedgwick County in the future. I live within 1,000 feet of the proposed TED Project in the annexed portion of the City of Clearwater, so this proposal directly affects my family. However, my opposition extends beyond my own property. I do not believe industrial solar facilities belong anywhere in Sedgwick County. This county is home to the largest city in Kansas, its growing rural communities, productive farmland, and 1,000s of families. We should not be turning our county into an industrial energy corridor.”
Ms. Roubideaux
continued, “My particular neighborhood is covered on two sides by this, and I’m just going to list several concerns here. Again, I’m not going to elaborate on some of them. The Clearwater opposition: the City of Clearwater Council opposes the TED Project as well. In addition, more than 1,200 residents have voiced their opinions to this TED Project. This project will hinder the growth and development of Clearwater. Clearwater City Council has identified multiple concerns about moving forward with industrial solar projects in our area. Growth is happening in southern Sedgwick County. Every road leading south of Wichita has new homes going up. It’s a beautiful part of the county: country roads, open fields, gorgeous sunsets with lots of wildlife. We don’t want to look outside to the prison fences. It’ll ruin the way that we live and our possible health in the future. That’s unknown how it’s affected at this point.”
Ms. Roubideaux
continued, “Also, the TED Project directly borders two residential neighborhoods within the City of Clearwater’s annexed area, which is the future growth pattern. Industrial solar scale facilities should not be placed immediately adjacent to established neighborhoods. We do not want to hear or see the industrial solar facility every day while looking out our windows for the rest of our lives. We built in the country for a reason. It is peaceful, beautiful, and a growing community. We have gathered more than 100 petitions from landowners within 1,000 feet of the TED Project who oppose it. We turned in protest petitions for 71 percent of the property against the proposed project. Under this proposal, 4 to 6 families stand to make a substantial financial gain while more than 100 families could lose quality of life and property values we spent years to achieve.”
Ms. Roubideaux
continued, “Let me also add that two of the families of the subject properties have family that is also in the petitions against the project. Anyway, I will close with this, even though I could go on for a long time. Mr. Howerton
said, “I’m coming at this a little different. You guys have been bombarded with all kinds of information, reports, technical testimonies, and so on. Today, I’m here to ask a few common sense questions that I think a lot of taxpayers and ratepayers would like answered. We’re often told that these projects will benefit our communities, but can anyone guarantee that they will lower our electric rates or even keep them from increasing? The honest answer is no. No utility, no developer, and no regulator can guarantee our electric rates will remain stable because future rates depend on many factors, including transmission costs, grid improvements, inflation, maintenance, and future regulatory decisions.”
Mr. Howerton
continued, “My concern is about the people that can least afford these increases, and believe me, there will be rate increases when these projects come online. Many of our seniors live on fixed incomes. Many families already struggle to pay their monthly electric bill. Even a modest increase in electric rates can mean difficult choices between paying for electricity, buying groceries, or even purchasing medication. If these projects are truly in the public’s best interest, then the public deserves clear answers. Has an independent economic study been completed showing the expected impact of these projects will have on residential electric rates over the next 10, 20, or 30 years? Can anyone guarantee that county residents will see lower bills because of these projects? If those promised benefits never materialize, who bears the financial burden, the developer or the ratepayer? Past experience suggests the ratepayer will bear the burden. The power company incurs costs to provide access to the grid in addition to cost of purchasing electricity and applying its profit margin. Ratepayers lose again.”
Mr. Howerton
continued, “Before moving forward with these projects of this magnitude and approving similar applications in the future, I ask the citizens of Sedgwick County receive transparent, independent, and honest answers. One more thing I’d like to add before I close. These companies are coming in. They’re not doing this out of the goodness of their hearts. They’re doing this to make money, and that’s what they’re here for. They’ll go anywhere where they can make a dime. Yes, it may help, but again, in the long run, it is probably going to hurt the ratepayers. Today, I would urge you guys to please deny this project. Thank you very much.”

We aren’t, and we can’t operate at a discounted admission price. We’ve even granted electric companies to come through our land with electricity poles to provide electricity to new development. We’re thankful to live in America where property owners have rights. We have followed the rules the county laid out, and we as property owners have chosen to lease our ground for solar. Now with a no vote, the government is taking away our rights, even though the rules have been followed, and we plan to use this lease money to continue to benefit and support our community. Please vote in support of Branch Line Solar.”

Mr. Metcalf
said, “So I would have to refer to that. The standard practice is to get a third party engineer who has the qualifications to put together that estimate. Depending on what you include and don’t include, that estimate can vary. If the county wants to be more restrictive, you should not include the salvage value of the steel and the posts, but let’s be honest, there’s a lot of salvage value in that steel. It would be sold as scrap and be put into good use. So, if you want to have a higher decommissioning cost, you don’t let the salvage value offset it, and you can use a multiplier, say 120 percent of the estimate from the professional engineer to get that. I would have to defer to my team if on this project and this specific decommissioning plan if, the methodology that we used.”
Mr. Metcalf
continued, “The standard and best practice that I’ve seen is a third party engineer mutually agreeable that readdresses it every five years so you can account for inflation. I think decommissioning bonds are a really good thing. We’ve developed a lot of energy infrastructure in this country. A lot of that didn’t have decommissioning bonds, and there’s the legacy of tens of thousands of abandoned oil wells. We’ve developed projects in that area with oil wells that have now been plugged because of our projects. I think any responsible infrastructure development should have a decommissioning bond. So, I would, happy to elaborate more. It’s a topic that I feel strongly about, and I think it’s a good thing.”

Mr. Wadle

said, “Okay.”
Mr. Wadle

Commissioner Baty
said, “As I mentioned, well, several hours ago in other conversations, we have built policy, and I have a lot of heartburn when we create policy on a host of different things, particularly in zoning, and we communicate to the community, this is our policy. Landowners, this is our policy. Developers, this is our policy. Then we defer from the policy. I have a lot of heartburn with that. Whether I like or dislike, we built a policy for a reason. On the restrictions in urban area of growth, remind me the heart of what we were trying to accomplish. I’m not asking your opinion in as much as, this is the struggle is because the heart is to help cities with their growth. We did not want to be prohibitive in their growth. What we’re doing in unincorporated Sedgwick County, we did not want to be at odds with our cities. Just because this isn’t in the urban area of growth, how should we interpret this? I’m having a hard time here.”
Mr. Wadle
said, “Okay. Well, the urban growth area, first off, what it is is it’s an area where a community is likely to grow and be able to provide services in the future.”
Commissioner Baty
said, “Sure.”
Mr. Wadle
said, “I can’t remember what the exact number is, but maybe it’s like 20 years into the future, but there’s a general time frame. What is the process, and how you get those, and how they get expanded is that there needs to be evidence provided to the MAPC. The Planning Commission kind of weighs this and ultimately it’s something that the County Commission would consider based on a recommendation, but there’s evidence. There’s information that goes into that. It’s a likely growth area. So, that’s one thing.”
Mr. Wadle
continued, “The second thing is that when solar regulations were developed, you’ll remember that there was a project that had been discussed, and the regulations were not crafted around this project, but it was information that, context that people kind of were aware of. It was located between two communities, and it was located in an area that was sited for an industrial park and some other things. As part of those regulations, there was also an update to the Comprehensive Plan with guidance language about solar facilities. Commissioner Baty
said, “Okay.”
Mr. Wadle
said, “Like that was already information that was in there. That was a guideline. Then the question came, are the current regulations really a good fit for the county still? I think one of the, and partially that was answered by using the urban growth areas.”

Mr. Waggoner
said, “That’s a good question, Commissioner Baty. First, I might start by clarifying, and Scott was getting into it, how the 2035 Urban Growth Areas Map is defined in the Comprehensive Plan. As you might guess by me saying 2035, it depicts the anticipated long-term growth patterns for the cities of Sedgwick County. These areas are not prescriptive or binding in nature, but serve as a reasonable indication as to where future efficient and fiscally responsible extension of public infrastructure, services, and corporate limits could occur by 2035. So at this point, it’s a nine-year window into the future.”
Mr. Waggoner
continued, “I think Commissioner Baty, there’s a number of ways you can look at this, but when you adopted a code two years ago, when this Board did, you allowed where these can be considered. You made urban growth areas off-limits. I think you could reasonably look at this and look at a period longer than the nine years. Of course, this application that we’re considering here is proposed to be much longer than that. I think you can also weigh the city’s request on this matter, and you judge it against your code.”

I’m just afraid to handcuff the whole area for 30 years. So, I’m going to make a motion.”
MOTION
Chairman Blubaugh moved to override the recommendation of the MAPC, deny the zone change for the following reasons listed with the Golden factors and the subsequent findings to support the motion. The character of the neighborhood is not consistent with the proposed uses. The zoning and uses of nearby properties are not consistent with the proposed use. Lastly, there’s a significant neighborhood opposition to the application. The following findings are made.
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Justin is that still 71 percent or has that changed?”
Mr. Waggoner
said, “I believe that’s still the same. I’m looking at Scott Wadle, and he’s nodding that it is.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay.”
MOTION (CONTINUED)
There’s significant neighborhood opposition as reflected by the protest petitions that amount to approximately 71 percent protest in the notice area in the case, the testimony at the MAPC meeting which was primarily opposed to the application, and the number of comments against the application received today. The character of the neighborhood is a mixture of agricultural uses and single-family housing, a significant portion of which is not tied to agricultural uses. The application would introduce a large-scale use for a solar energy conversion system that would be akin to the industrial use. This would not be consistent with the existing character of the neighborhood. Nearly all of the nearby property in the unincorporated area is zoned Rural Residential and is utilized for agricultural purposes and single-family housing. There is also property annexed into the City of Clearwater that is zoned and used for single-family houses.
The proposed Conditional Use contemplates a use that is significantly more intense than those in nearby properties, making the zoning and uses out of step with those already found in a portion of unincorporated Sedgwick County. The application area is currently zoned as Rural Residential purposes. Denial of the application would enable the property to continue to be utilized for agricultural and residential purposes, which would continue to be viable uses for the property. Nearby properties run the risk of having the property valuations negatively impacted if the application would be approved. A study completed by Virginia Tech in 2025 indicated that residential houses located within three miles of the solar site would experience a 4.8 percent reduction in value.
There are several homes located along the periphery of this application and well within the three miles, so this application would present some concern of reduced property valuation. This risk is heightened by the fact that there are multiple residential subdivisions that adjoin the application area. The application borders the City of Clearwater’s Urban Growth Area as identified within the Comprehensive Plan. In fact, this application borders properties that are part of the City of Clearwater within the Comprehensive Plan. Clearwater’s urban growth area is identified as a growth area through 2035, for the next nine years. The solar application has been proposed to last between 35 to 50 years. If approved, this solar application could act as an impediment to northern growth of the City of Clearwater for up to half a century. Given that Clearwater is located to the south of Wichita and many Clearwater residents may work in Wichita and travel there for other reasons, approval of this application could jeopardize the viability of Clearwater’s future growth opportunities in the direction where the growth would be most likely to occur.
It is also found that the opposition from the City of Clearwater as stated within their City of Clearwater City Council approved resolution number 7-2026 reflects concerns that this application, if approved, may interfere with or hinder future growth and development which is a relevant consideration and further support the view that this application is not in conformance with the Comprehensive Plan. It is found that the aesthetics of rows upon rows of solar, which will most likely have a certain level of visibility even with the screening that is contemplated, will not align with the rural characteristics of the area around the application.
Commissioner Wise seconded the motion.
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “I have a motion and a second. I am now opening it up for discussion before we vote. Commissioners, would you like to discuss?”
Commissioner Meitzner

So I’m kind of the opposite corner of the county. So I don’t quite have the feel that you’d have. So, I’ll probably be supportive of the motion.”

said, “Okay. Commissioner Baty.”

Commissioner Baty
continued, “I was going to hopefully create some modifications, tighten some things up, but I’ve read the room rather effectively I think. I understand where we’re at right now. I’m going to be a no vote to the motion for the same reason as earlier is that I know that our farmers are hurting. I understand that our farmers need to have options on their land, economic options on their land. So I understand the challenge. I understand, and this is not an endorsement of solar or an endorsement of industrial use. This is an endorsement of landowner rights. It’s an endorsement of our farmers and our property owners. We’ve got work to do to make sure that everybody’s on the same page so that people all throughout Sedgwick County understand the rules in which we work by and the policies.”
Commissioner Baty
continued, “There are tensions here with the City of Clearwater. I fully understand that. I see that, and the heart of the policy, what it was intended to do, I see the tension. I fully see the tension. When it comes to decisions like this, this comes down to principles and the principle that I’m going to lean into is the principle of property rights and defending the rights and the decision-making of our property owners and our farmers. So I will vote no with appreciation, and respect, and understanding of the reasons why many in this room are asking us to oppose this project and Commissioners that are asking to do the same. Commissioner Meitzner
said, “Can I just add that if this motion carries, and it needed four votes to do so, but that does trigger that the applicant can come and reapply within a year or even six months if it drastically changes some. So, okay.”
Chairman Blubaugh
said, “Okay. Go ahead and call the roll please.”
